Nvidia today held its first all-hands meeting since the resignation of co-founder and former CEO Jensen Huang. According to Wired, interim CEO Colette Kress offered more money and equity on a faster-than-expected schedule to calm staffers' nerves."So I’m here to tell you, the best thing that we want to do is to take care of each and every single employee that is here and to make sure that you feel good about this company and you do not worry about your comp,” Wired reports Kress said.The pay and equity bumps are set for December, far ahead of the company's normal raise cycle. Keeping current employees happy will be one of Kress' most important short-term challenges. A potential exodus of talent could also spook investors, despite standout earnings on Thursday. A leadership crisis could make the company seem less stable, and Huang had placed himself as the anchor point for many decisions and the company's overall vision.Huang has expressed interest in returning to Nvidia, saying he would be "honored" to lead the company again if asked by the board. The board was clear in yesterday's earnings call that it did not intend to bring Huang back, though there is reportedly a pro-Huang faction.Nvidia cofounder Chris Malachowsky was also on the call, explaining that the company lives its values rather than simply plastering them on posters.“We’re going to be fine,” he said.

#10400. One UI 9.5 Beta is out now for these Galaxy devices – how to install it

Samsung’s beta train has officially left the station, so to speak. One UI 9.5 is starting to roll out to these devices, and with it, some major changes to its Galaxy phones.

There are a couple of reasons the One UI 9.5 beta packs a substantial punch on your Galaxy S26. For one, it’s a halfway mark between Android versions where more useful features can be implemented. Samsung started this trend of releasing the next half-step up in One UI versions with its standard flagships. That’s why, when the Galaxy S27 becomes official, it will launch with One UI 9.5 preloaded.

Samsung details some of the key changes we’ll see in the One UI 9.5 beta, like a new journaling app and better video/camera controls.

It might be because the Galaxy S27 is the first device slated for the stable One UI 9.5 launch, but the Galaxy S26, Galaxy S26 Plus, and Galaxy S26 Ultra are the only devices that can access the beta update. The list doesn’t include the Galaxy Z Fold 8 or Fold 8 Ultra, even though they’ve become some of the best devices Samsung has to offer.

Samsung also puts limitations on regions, noting the program will only be open in Germany, India, Korea, Poland, the UK, and the US. Users on other devices outside of those regions won’t be able to download the beta.

That isn’t to say they’re locked out forever. Samsung will very likely expand the beta program before the Galaxy S27 launch, meaning more devices will get access and in wider areas.

On your Galaxy S26, S26 Plus, or S26 Ultra, you’ll need to sign up for the One UI beta program once more. This is an entirely new beta program, and it doesn’t look like participants carry over from the One UI 9 beta.

Once you finish that process, you should be all set with One UI 9.5 beta.

Once again, since it’s early access, you shouldn’t expect things to be perfect. If you find the experience is awful, you can always revert back.

Samsung published the full changelog alongside its OTA update, as outlined below.

Sports animations in Now barAnimation effects are now available for more sports. Celebrate your favorite team’s goals and victories with special visual effects. You can download these effects in Now bar settings.

Fingerprint and face recognition at the same time

#10398. YouTube Music rolling out corner Gemini icon to launch Ask Music

Besides removing the Samples tab, YouTube Music for Android and iOS is rolling out instant access to Ask Music with a new Gemini icon in the top-right corner.

The Gemini spark appears on almost every screen in the app, including the Home feed, Library tab, Now Playing, albums, and playlists. It matches Gemini in Gmail and other Workspace apps online, as well as Google Messages in a future update.

Tapping launches Ask Music, which requires a YouTube Premium or Music Premium subscription, as a fullscreen sheet. This chatbot lets you create a mix, discover artists, find podcasts, build a playlist, and ask about your listening history. You can also “customize your queue or dive deeper into artist lore.” In addition to the text field, there’s also voice input.

Ask Music launched in 2024 and was previously accessed from a card in the Home feed.

The new access point has been rolling out in recent days as a server-side update, and we’re seeing it on many more devices as of today. YouTube previewed this addition back in September along with “your podcast lineup.” This “personalized AI-audio guide” appears in the Podcasts feed to provide a “quick, weekly spoken preview of recommended shows and digs into why you’ll like them, before jumping into the suggested episodes.”

#10397. Find Orphan Pages and Buried Content: Build an Internal Link Graph for Your Site

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

The pages of a site form a graph. Think of it as many nodes connected to each other via edges. Each of the edges are the links between the pages, and the nodes themselves are the pages. Now if you start to visualize this as a graph, you will quickly see that there are multiple ways to traverse this graph and reach a particular endpoint.

A search crawler usually uses one of these pages as a starting point and crawls through all of the pages (especially when pages are not fully covered in a sitemap).

If a page lies very deep from the home page, it means that it's not an important page. It's an indication to the search engine that this is not something that you yourself consider important, and the search engine has a lesser incentive to rank this page over pages that are linked better within your site.

The key metric here is link depth. That is the number of clicks it takes to reach a particular page from your home page.

Another key metric is broken links. If you have too many broken links, it indicates to the search engine that your site is outdated and not well maintained, and the search engine will then not want to rank your site.

The way to identify both these problems is the same: to actually walk through your site, do a crawl of your site outside in, and try to figure out what the link depth is and if there are broken links on your website.

Seed the crawl with the home page and all URLs mentioned in the sitemap. Technically, the sitemap is not required if you're doing a crawl, but going from the sitemap will allow us to detect orphaned pages as well.

Fetch the pages and follow redirects as required. Record when you're following a redirect: we do not let fetch follow it silently, we record each hop and add its target to the crawl. Record HTTP errors as they occur.

Drop URL fragments and tracking parameters. Keep trailing slashes because sometimes there can be mismatches around expectation of trailing slashes in different URLs of a site.

In order to navigate from the home page, we can use BFS. So we know that the first time we reach a page, that is the page's shortest path.

We can cut off the depth over a certain threshold, say 5, to indicate that pages beyond that are too far or too deep to be reached.

Noindex pages will still be crawled. However, we need to make a record of them being not indexed, to reflect that they will not be present in the search engine.

The desired page depth is as close to the home page as possible. Important pages should be close to the home page. The deeper you go from the home page, it signals to the search engine that the page is less important.

A couple of examples of how a deep page is present:

The suggested fix for this is that we link the pages from as close to the home page as possible:

Wasted links are those internal links that do not do the job they're supposed to do. The different types of wasted links:

We have seen here how to look at our own website through the lens of a search engine. You can use some of the code over here to create your own crawler and identify internal linking issues; the full script is in this gist. Alternatively, we can use a free internal link visualizer to identify these issues and focus only on the fixes to improve our site's internal linking.

#10396. Nvidia GeForce driver bug reportedly hammers SSDs with up to 30GB of writes per hour

That would use up to 5% of a typical 1TB SSD’s rated endurance each year.

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

Nvidia’s GeForce driver 621.14 is reportedly causing excessive background writes on idle PCs, inducing extra wear on SSDs. One user measured about 3GB an hour on the susceptible driver, and another mentioned estimated up to 30GB/hour on the newer 621.24. This follows a similar-looking bug from AMD’s video driver discovered last month.

The bug is described by user Andergraude in an Nvidia forum post as “new in 621.14,” which came out on Nov. 4, who says it operates by “continuously [rewriting] the binary log file” with “real writes” to the drive. The writes are coming from the NVDisplay.Container.exe process, or the “NVIDIA Display Container LS” service, which logs to the nvlog.nvlgstg file. This background service is necessary for running the Nvidia Control Panel and App. It’s not clear what’s written in the log, and not every system is necessarily impacted.

The “real writes” point comes from the fact that a per-process write counter is not the same as writes reaching the drive. The measurements came from Windows Resource Monitor, Windows performance counters, and CrystalDiskInfo. The Data Units Written counter matched Windows’ reporting. This test showed 515MB of process I/O coming with 270MB of total writes to the C: drive over five minutes. That works out to roughly 3GB per hour, in line with the user’s own estimate.

Worse, “the write volume increases as the file grows.” Stopping the service and deleting the log can temporarily reduce the writes. With the previous driver version 621.01, the writes totaled less than 2MB per five minutes, and a second user in the thread confirmed that a rollback helps.

Mitigation attempts, such as three clean reinstalls with the help of DDU from that second user, did not fix the problem. The writing process also impacted gaming and video performance, potentially causing hitching, which suggests it gets worse under load.

Another user, AyzekUorren, in a separate thread about driver 621.24, which came out Nov. 12, says that it could get up to “about 30gb per 1h” or even “70gb or even more” with longer sessions. This is even with telemetry disabled in the Nvidia App. Noted is that every Alt+Tab added 500MB to 1GB of writes, at least for this user.

In AMD’s case, it was the External Events Utility, or atieclxx.exe, that exhibited constant writes on an idle PC. In that case, the AMD utility was reportedly copying its own executable every second. To put the impact into context, the AMD bug had rates reported between 0.3 and 5MB/s while total drive writes in the 621.14 test measured around 0.9MB/s.

Get Tom's Hardware's best news and in-depth reviews, straight to your inbox.

The Nvidia numbers are somewhat alarming, given that a typical 1TB SSD is rated for 600TB of writes over a standard five-year warranty. Even at 270MB per five minutes, this could be about 28TB per year — for a never-sleeping PC — which is reaching toward 5% of the drive’s rated endurance, although the writes include everything the system is writing and realistic uptime will be less. However, at the higher end — 30GB per hour, at eight hours a day — one could see 15% of drive endurance worn every year for nothing.

This doesn’t make it a drive killer, but given how much users value drive endurance, especially with SSD prices as they are, avoiding unnecessary writes like this is certainly a priority. To see if you’re affected, check Resource Monitor and look for the telltale NVDisplay.Container.exe and the nvlog.nvlgstg file in the list. You can also check the SMART Data Units Written value with CrystalDiskInfo or equivalent, such as your drive’s SSD toolbox.

At this time, no fix or hotfix has been announced, and the best workaround is to do a rollback. A similar issue in the past, fixed with driver update 471.96 back in 2021, was covered by Nvidia’s release notes. As it stands, Nvidia has “N/A” for 621.24’s Open Issues, and the company has not acknowledged the bug. For the AMD bug, the driver wasn't fixed for over three weeks (and came as an optional update).

#10392. Agent Distraction

What is easy for humans to skim past in code, agents see as a call to action. They do not know better, latching on to that which is littered across the files read during the planning and research phase of the coding journey.

As agents' agency through tools and skills grows the ability for side-quests scales proportionally. Obsolete specs, long resolved bugs, and lagging documentation no longer rest in long forgotten corners of the codebase, but rather are unintentionally ingested as context indirecting and misdirecting the next wave of coders - the agents. This indirection is not free: in the best case it only costs tokens, steps, and time - in the worst case it distracts and grounds the agent on falsehoods leading to incorrect logic and decisions during implementation.

Measuring how often agents fall into these traps across millions of monthly sessions is impossible with manual human review - we need an objective signal derivable from the code itself. Inline TODO(bug/...) comments provide the perfect proxy. TODOs tend to be left behind long after the underlying bug is resolved or abandoned, while the linked bug ticket acts as the canonical record. By cross-referencing every TODO an agent encounters against the TODO's bug status and the user's prompt, we can automatically separate valid work from wasted detours at scale.

By leveraging the distributed traces of agent interactions across thousands of engineers and over hundreds of millions of individual agent steps, we quantify the impact of TODO distractions in the codebase and how agents act on them:

Controlling for developer, codebase complexity, task type, model tier, number of files read, initial prompt length, and the step where the bug reference appeared, using a multi-way fixed-effects regression with developer-clustered standard errors, we see that unprompted bug detours add substantial overhead (all stat sig p<0.0001). Per session:

The Obsolete Detour: In the following trajectory we can observe how this drift plays out. During an investigation into an unrelated bug The agent views SurfaceControlTest.java and sees // TODO(bug/XXX)

This bug, while fixed in 2023, remains lingering in the codebase leading to subsequent investigation distraction across Steps with the agent digging into the bug to learn more.

The Eager Agent: In the trajectory the agent encountered a TODO in the bug it was investigating and decided (unprompted) to "fix" the code

The agent executed multiple Code Search and internal knowledge search queries for the bug ultimately resulting in the agent changing the ModelDownloaderV1 code. Later in the trajectory the human challenged the scope creep.

With the model when admitting it was an incorrect increase in scope:

While in both of these cases they were only trivial to minor detours - they were real distractions costing time, tokens, and human intervention.

As part of addressing this finding at scale we created agents that scrubbed the totality of our codebases, finding bug references in the code that were marked as fixed or obsolete for cleanup. That step alone however was not enough to determine if the TODOs in the codebase were load-bearing or not. The agents had to go further looking at the history of the bug, the file, and changes to the codebase, at times spanning years of history. Only in cases where the agent was able to confirm that the TODO and the intent behind the TODO was obsolete did our agent send a change for review to delete it from the codebase. As a result of this multi-layer approach, our todo cleaner has been able to remove thousands of actually obsolete references over the last 6 months exceeding 95% acceptance from human reviewers.

In recap, as we task agents on more and more ambitious problems, "agent friendly codebases" require more than just the code in the hotpath being healthy. The forgotten corners of the codebase now more than ever carry a significant tax levied against agents.

Tooling like presubmit analyzers, entire corpus scanners, and automated coding agents to remediate dead-todos offer out of the box ready to scale solutions to address the tax enabling a healthier and distraction free codebase for agents to thrive.

Projects that once were on the backburner to cleanup lingering debt but deprioritized due to perceived low value need to be reevaluated with the new agents acting at scale on your codebase.

The company said it 'values its interactions with gamers above all else'

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

AMD Japan's X account has been met with sharp reproach after claiming the company loves gamers and values its interactions with gamers above all else, with commenters and even a community note expressing dissent at the sentiment, owing to AMD's comments on its "data center-first" ethos and a commitment to AI that has driven the price of consumer PC gaming up to nearly unaffordable levels.

Quoting a Tom's Hardware post on the X social network discussing CEO Dr. Lisa Su meeting gamers and autographing a GPU, the AMD Japan account was quick to seize the moment. "AMD values its interactions with gamers above all else," the post read, but it was met with a stark reminder that gamers don't feel the same way. The post soon received a new community note that pointed out that AMD's own CEO doesn't agree. In fact, Su was quoted as saying that AMD is now "a data center-first company," with the note also pointing to the fact that AMD's data center revenue increased 97% year-over-year, as per its Q3 2026 earnings report. That equates to around $8.5 billion in revenue, while client and gaming revenue fell 8% to $3.7 billion. Gaming alone was down 40%.

At the time, Su said that AMD expected its server revenue to double year over year in 2027, with its data center AI revenue growing at an even faster rate. "As a result, we expect data center to be more than eighty percent of our total revenue in 2027 and to drive the majority of our revenue and earnings growth," the CEO said during the company's earnings call.

ゲーマーとの交流を何よりも大切にするAMD。 コミュニティと直接つながり、その声に耳を傾け続ける姿勢こそAMDの強みです。 AMD loves gamers. 🎮November 17, 2026

By comparison, the same call heard Su say that, in 2027, AMD was "planning for PC market unit shipments to decline by a double-digit percentage." With this all in mind, it's perhaps no surprise that the AMD Japan X account stirred such a strong response from gamers who feel that AMD has left them behind in search of a more lucrative data center market. AMD is certainly not alone in that regard. Nvidia, once the darling of the PC component enthusiast, is now focused on AI data center components instead. The company's high-performance AI chips are so capable that they're export-controlled, something that, among other reasons, will put former CEO Jensen Huang in front of a House Foreign Affairs panel in December.

As for AMD, its ill-timed, misjudged X post ended with "AMD loves gamers." Gamers would appear to disagree.

#10389. Fitbit Edge after 30 days: Life in the middle lane [Video]

The biggest compliment you can pay Google at the moment is that alongside the Pixel Watch, recent Fitbit releases have been a huge return to form – the Fitbit Edge included.

I need to nail my colors to the mast; I’m a Fitbit Air fanboy. I’ve talked about it multiple times on the Pixelated podcast, here on the site, and it made me switch away from the Pixel Watch 5 because of how easily it fit into my existence without insisting on itself.

The Fitbit Edge worried me at first because it comes with a screen, which is something I realized isn’t that important to me day-to-day. After 30 days of wear, I’m conflicted because maybe I was too hasty to ditch a display on my wrist.

The design is somewhat in a halfway house because it feels like it’s trying to be both a classic Fitbit while integrating some of the Pixel Watch features we know and love. Initially, I loved it, but it has become something that isn’t quite enough in either direction to suit my own tastes.

My love for the simplicity of the Fitbit Air has colored how I feel, but the Edge has an excellent core design. The display bezels remind me heavily of the original Pixel Watch; yes, they’re there, but you only notice them when the light catches the frame edge.

After a month of daily wear, the display has lost a bit of its luster. The oleophobic coating is just about holding up, but now we’re into the cooler months; it’ll be put through a barrage of jacket cuffs and hoodie sleeves. This is one of the only durability concerns I have had so far.

Despite that admittedly minor concern, the Fitbit Edge is not something you need to baby. I’ve bashed it with weights, caught my wrist on zips (zippers for you, my American friends), and bashed it around with reckless abandon. It’s solid and hasn’t picked up as much as a single scuff. I don’t care too much if a fitness tracker gets a beating, whereas I would be more concerned about damaging my Pixel Watch.

If you come in wanting a smartwatch-like experience from the Fitbit Edge, I think you’ll instantly be frustrated by the UI. It can feel cramped when you see notifications, and the large bezels don’t help all that much, but Google has well optimized almost all of the key sections outside of this.

Tiles are very well implemented; they’re just a smaller, squatter take on the Pixel Watch tile system, and it’s one of the best things about the Fitbit Edge interface. It’s also really snappy, which is something that isn’t the case with cheaper fitness trackers such as this. I’m constantly checking the weather or using the Google Health tile, but I have loved the quick access complication on the default watch face.

I tried a few of the other designs over the past 30 days; a lot of them are great. The default has become my favorite. It’s information-dense without being too overloaded. Tapping the complications takes you to the exact data point. I genuinely love how fuss-free an experience it is, save for a few minor complaints.

We could criticize the accuracy of the data or the lack of tracking for certain types of workouts, but I think that misses the point of the Fitbit Edge. It’s a simple tracker with a screen, and the fact that you get even basic ECG on a device of this size is impressive.

I truly have worn the Edge daily for a full month, and while the bands are comfortable, I have found that they cause me to get a rash on the underside of my left wrist just under the palm. It’s actively stopping me from wearing it all the time now. I hope that the new connector doesn’t slow down third-party development for bands, because I’m desperate for a fabric band to alleviate this issue.

This is exacerbated when the rubber band gets wet or sweaty. If you have sensitive skin, you might have the same problem as I have. Using some moisturizer has helped, but it is something I wasn’t prepared to suffer with before the Fitbit Edge turned up. I get that this is likely a “me” problem, but I wanted to note it.

When the screen gets wet or moist, it can bug out quite a lot. Taps don’t register properly, and a few water droplets can activate all the menus and submenus. Luckily, it won’t force your phone to place a call or anything like that, but the water protection mode is a must. I have kept it on extensively.

Swimmers will probably get super frustrated; heck, I was on a rainy day as the screen kept bugging out. Overall, though, I have enjoyed the fitness tracking. Unlike on the Fitbit Air, it is nice to have a screen to start a workout rather than head over to my phone. That said, the experience is the same as on your phone, but scaled down to fit the watch face.

I dislike that you don’t get a phone notification to show the current workout progress by default, like the Fitbit Air. I had become accustomed to that. Luckily, you can enable it in the Google Health settings. I like having this as I forget to stop workouts so often without a prompt to hit “stop.”

With audio playback, you’re also getting the partial benefits of watch-phone controls, but it’s a two-step process unlike on Pixel Watch. You either have to go to the “Media” app or have the tile active and navigate to it to access play/pause and all your other streaming app controls. I tend not to bother using these as it’s finicky.

This had me thinking about NFC. I think a lot of people would have liked NFC for Google Wallet on the Fitbit Edge, but I think having to enter a PIN on such a small screen would really have been a nightmare. I’ve made wireless payments with my watch less than five times, as it’s just easier to use my phone rather than contort my wrist to a wireless payment terminal. I figure this was something that was thought about and makes a lot of sense given the constraints.

One of my other complaints is that the DND and Bedtime modes from my account haven’t synced or at least don’t appear to sync. Unless I’m mistaken, you need to activate these settings manually every time, which has been super annoying when in bed, as a flick of your wrist can blind you in a dark room. I figure this is something that can be fixed with an update, so here’s hoping.

I’ve been super impressed with the battery life. A week is easily attainable for me with up to three gym visits per week, so I’ve charged 5 times in a month. That, to me, is excellent. I picked up a cheap charging dock, as the Edge uses the same charger for the Fitbit Air, so I figured why not.

It’s not the best-looking dock, but I quite like how it airs off my Edge so it’s not as stinky after gym sessions – as I wipe with an alcohol pad and clean as regularly as possible.

The experience is fine; it’s almost a third of the price of the Pixel Watch and lacks a few features due to the various compromises – compromises I can deal with, as smartwatches have become information overload for me over time, and I like that the app selection is ultra-refined. At no point does the Fitbit Edge give you information overload.

Coming from a Charge 6 or an older Fitbit, this is an amazing replacement. It’s the new “default” fitness tracker for people who want all the hallmarks of what makes a great Fitbit.

There is just enough. Nothing more, nothing less. To me, this feels like the perfect middle ground for a lot of people. You want something simple, touchscreen-enabled, and that doesn’t get in the way. As impressed as I have been with the Edge, though, I’ve loved a fuss-free tech accessory so much recently that I’ll probably go back to the Fitbit Air.

#10387. Vivo X500 Pro camera review: Battle of the small(er) phones vs Pixel 11 Pro

The compact Vivo X500 Pro has some camera downgrades compared to the Pro Max, so are you better off saving money and getting the base Vivo X500 instead?

Over the past few years, Vivo has made a name for itself as the camera powerhouse smartphone company. The first time I used a Vivo X100 Pro blew my mind in a way smartphone cameras hadn’t for a few years, but does the company’s 4th iteration — yes, Vivo skipped the X400 — of the X series still impress?

The answer is mostly yes. The Vivo X500 series includes five models: the base X500, the X500 Pro, and the X500 Pro Max. Unlike some other phones, all three of these models feature different camera hardware that takes advantage of the physical size differences. The X500 Pro Max’s hardware is the best of the bunch, but that phone is substantially larger and heavier as a result.

So I’m comparing the more reasonably sized models to see which semi-compact Vivo phone you should consider this year. I tossed the Google Pixel 11 Pro into the comparison since Google also offers a compact flagship camera phone.

Most camera specs between these phones are surprisingly comparable, so the differences you’re about to see are largely caused by some pretty massive differences in processing. I already covered Vivo’s wild new AI camera mode, so let’s go for something a little closer to normal smartphone camera use: a day at the Biltmore.

Perhaps the biggest difference between these phones is Vivo’s pursuit of “perfection,” while Google seems to embrace a more “natural” look. At 10x and 5x, the photos from both Vivo phones are essentially noise-free, have clear sharpening applied to them, and are generally quite vibrant. Despite having similar specs, the Vivo X500 Pro’s more advanced Sony LYT telephoto sensor pulls out more detail.

Google’s images are notably softer, although not in a low-resolution sort of way. They look more like they’re taken from a standard camera rather than a smartphone, which some people may prefer. While telephoto image quality between the Vivo X500 Pro and Google Pixel 11 Pro is mostly down to preference, Google’s phone is substantially faster while taking the actual photo. In all of the darker shots, the Pixel 11 Pro took the picture instantly. Both Vivo X500 models took a 2-3 second exposure shot, making me stand there for longer than I’m used to.

Ultrawide performance is where I feel we see the largest gap between these phones. Both the Vivo X500 and X500 Pro cameras offer bright, well-balanced shots that are vibrant but not unrealistic. The light sources in the room emit a lovely warm glow, yet the bulbs themselves aren’t overblown. The X500 Pro’s slightly warmer image across all three scenarios looks best to me.

The Pixel 11 Pro’s ultrawide images are often underexposed and lacking in dynamic range. The staircase shot exhibits this problem the most. This can come off as natural in some scenarios, as modern smartphone photography is notorious for brightening dark areas too much, but too often the Pixel 11 Pro’s ultrawide shots just feel unnaturally dark.

The main camera is much more balanced, although it’s fun to push these phones to find their weaknesses. One of the best examples of differing priorities is in the wreath shot, which was quite dark in real life thanks to the bright sunlight outside. The Pixel 11 Pro valiantly tried to brighten up the shadows so you could actually see the wreath (since it’s clearly the subject), causing the rest of the scene to look forcefully bright.

The same issue cropped up in the image of the wine rack, where both Vivo phones captured a nicer-looking shot. The image of the trellis walkway is a tie, but the photo of my ridiculous bowl of ice cream looks best on the Vivo X500 Pro, once again.

Portrait mode is where I think the Vivo X500 Pro is the clear loser, but it’s not for any of the reasons in the other categories above. Rather, the X500 Pro seems to have some weird warping happening in the bokeh processing that’s not present on the base Vivo X500. This is in both front-facing and rear camera portrait shots, so it’s not a lens issue or some weird setting.

Google cleaned up its portrait shots so much this year that it’s borderline ridiculous. Pixels had the worst portrait mode for years, but this year’s overhaul of the portrait algorithm made all the difference in the world.

Overall, the Vivo X500 series is another solid release for Vivo, and while neither the base nor Pro models offer the same top-tier camera specs as the Pro Max model, it’s clear Vivo pulled off something great. The Vivo X500 Pro retails for €1,399 in the EU, while the base X500 global model is a little harder to find, but should be quite a bit cheaper if you can get your hands on it.

#10386. Running an AI Agent on a Local LLM: What I Measured on One GPU

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

Running an AI agent on your own GPU sounds simple: point it at Ollama and stop paying per token. I set up OpenClaw, an open-source AI assistant, with a 9B model in Ollama on one RTX 3090 and connected it to Discord. Then I read the Ollama log for every request.

The agent worked. The numbers were not what I expected, though. A one-line question turned into a prompt of more than 18,000 tokens, the first reply took over a minute, and the small model sometimes guessed instead of using its tools.

This article covers what I measured and what each number means if you plan to run an agent on local hardware.

OpenClaw ran in Docker and reached Ollama on the host at http://host.docker.internal:11434. Discord was the chat interface, and OpenClaw's web panel showed the sessions and context use.

This article is about the results, not the installation. The full step-by-step setup is in a separate guide to running OpenClaw in Docker with Ollama and Discord. That guide covers the Compose file, the Discord bot and how to keep the ports off your network.

All timings below come from the Ollama log on the test machine. Ollama loaded the model with a 32,768-token context.

My first message in Discord was short: "Which model are you running on, and what is 17 * 23?" The bot answered correctly: it ran on ollama/qwen3.5:9b, and 17 × 23 is 391.

The Ollama log showed what that question cost. The model processed 18,415 prompt tokens and generated 33 tokens.

The extra tokens come from the agent itself. With every request, OpenClaw sends:

On the RTX 3090, reading that prompt took 10 seconds at 1,838 tokens per second. A slower GPU, or a CPU, needs much longer to read the same prompt.

Prompt tokens per model call, from the Ollama log. Only the first message had to process the whole prompt.

OpenClaw counts tokens with its own estimate, which was higher than the Ollama count. The gateway log estimated the prompt at 24,608 tokens, against a prompt budget of 24,576 tokens for this model. By OpenClaw's own count, the base prompt alone used up the budget before any conversation history.

The web panel showed a similar figure: 68 to 69 percent of the context window was in use for the Discord sessions after only a few messages.

The first message arrived while the model was not loaded in GPU memory. That reply took 1 min 10 s:

Reply times on the test machine. Loading the model took most of the first reply.

The speed-up comes from prompt caching. The system prompt, tool definitions and workspace files at the start of the prompt stay the same between requests. Ollama kept that part cached and processed only the new tokens. The model wrote at 91 to 96 tokens per second in every test.

Ollama's documentation says models stay in memory for 5 minutes by default before they are unloaded. After that, the next message pays the load time again. You can change this with the OLLAMA_KEEP_ALIVE environment variable on the Ollama server, or with the keep_alive parameter per request. A negative value, such as -1, keeps the model loaded. I did not change this setting in the test.

If you run Ollama in Docker, pass the variable with -e OLLAMA_KEEP_ALIVE=-1 or in the environment: section of your Compose file.

Two things used the GPU besides my chat messages.

A heartbeat. OpenClaw's web panel listed one scheduled automation, heartbeat-main, which runs every 30 minutes. The agent wakes on that schedule, so the model gets called even when nobody chats. With Ollama's 5-minute default, the model is unloaded between heartbeats unless someone chatted recently, so a heartbeat most likely starts with a model load. I did not measure this.

An embedding model. During the first message, OpenClaw also called Ollama's /api/embed endpoint twice to index its memory. It used qwen3-embedding:4b, which was already installed on the Ollama server. I did not set OpenClaw to use that model; it picked it on its own.

The two models took this much GPU memory:

That is 11.4 GB for the models alone, almost half of the RTX 3090's 24 GB.

What this means for you: plan GPU memory for the chat model, the embedding model and the context, not the chat model alone. If the GPU also runs other models, the heartbeat can compete with them.

The first two tests in Discord: a question, then a file written with a tool. The user name is blurred.

The mistakes showed up when the model had to check something:

After the restart, the bot claimed it had lost the earlier session. When asked to use its read tool, it read the file correctly.

The fix for the first problem was simple. When I wrote "use your read tool to open homelab-test.md", the model opened the file and listed all three points correctly. Naming the tool made it check instead of guess.

The model listed four files and missed the test file and a folder, because it answered from its prompt.

Based on this test, a local 9B model on one consumer GPU works for:

If you need better answers, OpenClaw also works with hosted models, but then your prompts leave your machine.

In this test, qwen3.5:9b used 6.3 GB and the qwen3-embedding:4b memory model used 5.1 GB. Together the two models needed 11.4 GB.

The model had to load into GPU memory first, which took 59 seconds of the 70-second reply. Ollama keeps a model loaded for 5 minutes by default, and the OLLAMA_KEEP_ALIVE setting changes that.

Every request includes OpenClaw's system prompt, its tool definitions and its workspace files. A one-line question became an 18,415-token prompt in this test.

Yes. A heartbeat automation runs every 30 minutes and calls the model even when nobody sends a message.

It handled questions, arithmetic and file creation correctly in this test. It sometimes answered from its prompt instead of using its tools. When it wrongly said its memory was lost, asking it to use its read tool made it check the file and answer correctly.

#10385. DDR6 RAM shrinks by 44% while delivering 4 times more bandwidth than DDR5 — JEDEC previews 12.8 GT/s modules with up to 1 terabyte of capacity per slot

JEDEC puts next-gen DDR6 memory on a diet

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

Today's best RAM will look huge compared to upcoming DDR6 memory. JEDEC has just released a preview of the DDR6 specification, confirming that next-generation memory modules will be 44% shorter than DDR5. That is only the physical aspect, though. From a performance perspective, DDR6 will debut at 12.8 GT/s, more than double DDR5's baseline transfer rate, while also quadrupling the memory bandwidth per memory module thanks to a new design.

JEDEC's DDR6 preview highlights improvements in various areas, including performance, capacity, reliability, and power efficiency. These advancements align with the upgrades DDR5 brought to the table when it launched.

One of the most notable differences is that DDR6 memory modules will measure just 74mm in length, 44% shorter than DDR5’s 133mm memory modules. This makes DDR6 only 6% longer than DDR5 SO-DIMM modules, which you commonly find in laptops and mini-PCs.

JEDEC has maintained the pin count for many years, sticking to 288 pins for DDR4 and DDR5. However, DDR6 may finally break that mould. Given the substantial reduction in length with DDR6 and the data bus width upgrade, it is reasonable to assume that DDR6 will have a completely different pinout and density. Sadly, JEDEC has not revealed the pin count for DDR6.

Early rumors suggested that DDR6 may be limited to CAMM form factors to feed the AI boom. However, JEDEC's announcement at least does not rule out other form factors. It appears JEDEC is still determining which form factors DDR6 will use. Potential options include RDIMM, UDIMM, and CAMM formats, just like DDR5.

Baseline JEDEC Bandwidth Per Module (GB/s)

Peak Baseline Bandwidth Per Module (GB/s)

Notably, JEDEC has increased the data bus width in DDR6 to 96 bits, a 50% boost over DDR5's 64 bits. The channel architecture for DDR6 will also change substantially. While DDR4 and DDR5 leveraged a single 64-bit channel and two 32-bit sub-channels, DDR6 takes a different approach. JEDEC will implement a design with four 24-bit sub-channels.

Using the baseline for comparison, DDR6 offers up to 153.6 GB/s of total module bandwidth, compared to DDR5's 38.4 GB/s. That is a 4X uplift in bandwidth. If we compare DDR5-9200 RDIMMs, which max out at 73.6 GB/s per module, with DDR6-12800 RDIMMs, the uplift is a little more than double.

Get Tom's Hardware's best news and in-depth reviews, straight to your inbox.

At least on the server side, DDR6 RDIMMs will start at DDR6-12800, whereas DDR5 RDIMMs topped out at DDR5-9200. JEDEC anticipates DDR6 reaching DDR6-14400 and beyond. This is for the server and enterprise market, though. It remains to be seen which DDR6 speed will launch in the consumer market. DDR5 commenced at DDR5-4800.

DDR6 may be shorter than DDR5, but it makes up for it by having 2X the bank count. This will help push higher data rates and capacities. DDR6 will support 128Gb (16GB) dies, which represents a massive leap. We are looking at 4X the density of early DDR5 memory modules. Instead of placing chips side by side on the PCB, DDR6 enables die stacking, allowing manufacturers to place multiple dies on top of each other via Through-Silicon Vias (TSVs).

Consumer DDR5 memory modules currently sit at 64GB per module, while server RDIMMs have reached 512GB. DDR6 could raise module capacity to 128GB or 256GB and potentially scale RDIMMs to 1TB or more per slot.

DDR6 has no official launch date. Many believe 2029 is a realistic target for commercialization. However, in the current state of things, it would be a miracle if DDR6 had no setbacks.

#10383. Google Play wants Android apps to nail foldable basics by April 2027

Google Play is introducing new adaptive app and game quality requirements next year to elevate the Android foldable experience.

Starting April 2027, the Play Store will require developers to meet Tier 3 of the Android adaptive app quality guidelines for foldables.

Referred to as “Adaptive ready” or “basic” support, Tier 3 requires apps not to use letterboxing (or other compatibility modes) and to fill the entire screen. During rotation, resizing, and folding/unfolding, apps must preserve scroll position, text input, and media playback. Apps must also support multi-window environments.

Apps that do not meet this quality bar will no longer be able to publish or update on Google Play, and may have reduced visibility on the store.

At the same time, Google Play will require foldable device screenshots for app listings: “Providing foldable screenshots by April 2027 will become a standard submission requirement when publishing new apps or updating your store listing.”

Curious to see whether the requirements will actually be as strict as they make them seem (on paper).

If so, then also Google will have a ton of work to do to modernise all of their apps.

By August 2027, new and updated apps will be required to target Android 17 (API level 37).

Over time, this “quality framework will progressively expand to incorporate Tier 2 and Tier 1 standards across foldables, tablets, Googlebooks, and Android XR.”

Apps that meet Tier 2 (better) or Tier 1 (best) will get “premier store badging, editorial spotlighting, and enhanced merchandising across Google Play as the platform bar elevates.”

On the gaming front, titles will need to add foldable screenshots to listings by April 2027, while Tier 3 large screen quality guidelines go into effect in August 2027.

The Winds of Winter have arrived — just not written by G.R.R. Martin.

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

We've just had a few high-stakes security vulnerabilities making systems administrators run around with their heads on fire, and the deluge doesn't look like it's stopping any time soon. Java, the programming language that Oracle has always claimed is on billions of devices, has a critical security vulnerability that allows for remote code execution (RCE), often without even needing to log in to the application. The consequence is that an attacker can gain access to the target system, read user and internal data, and have a launch point for further shenanigans, including database access and potential system takeover.

The flaw was dubbed Java4Shell by Calif Research, the lab that found the vulnerability. The researchers say it rates 9.2 on the newer CVSS 4.0 scale, though individual reports from companies like Oracle itself and Red Hat give it their own ratings, but they're all in the 8 to 9.8 range depending on the vendor. The root cause is a bug in OpenJDK, the library that nearly every distribution of Java, whether Oracle's or anyone else's, derives their implementations from.

You can see an exploit of the bug in action below across multiple software services.

The actual bug is a flaw in an optimization for Base64 decoding, functionality that's ubiquitous in any programming language. Base64 is a format to allow for easy transformation of binary data into plain text. In the modern day, it's used to embed files in the middle of text, like inserting image data in the middle of HTML or CSS, attaching files to emails (internally they're all text-based), or simply representing data as text for visual inspection, like file checksums or cryptographic key fingerprints.

Oracle has already fixed the bug and issued updated versions of its wares. However, much like digital certificates, operating systems and even individual programs include their own copy of a Java executable, meaning that the potential blast radius is far wider than you'd expect at first glance. Calif listed many such common applications: at the time of the post, Keycloak, Jira, Confluence, Jenkins, and PingFederate all had full unauthenticated RCE, while Apache Kafka Broker, Solr, Elasticsearch, Gerrit, and Spinnaker have RCEs after authentication.

Because Java is so darn widespread, most every company has to issue security advisories and updates for their wares, including IBM, Red Hat, Siemens, and Broadcom. Because so many containers also use Java-based applications in some form, those too are vulnerable, and the count of individual vulnerable container images across registries everywhere is likely a high figure. This is likely not a bug that will be easily eradicated.

There is a condition to trigger the exploit, but it's not too difficult to fulfill. The Java instance in question must be running on an x86-64 CPU with AVX-512 support and the VBMI extension. In practice, that means chips from the Intel Ice Lake and AMD Zen 4 families, from 2021 and 2022 respectively, for their server variants. Older CPUs are not affected, as the code optimization that contains the bug is specific to VBMI.

Get Tom's Hardware's best news and in-depth reviews, straight to your inbox.

The specific bug is in a hand-written assembly optimization that fails a simple bounds check. If an attacker inputs 64 As and an "=" sign as padding, they can overwrite neighboring memory, and then weaponize that corruption, similar to how Heartbleed worked. As usual these days, Calif says that the vulnerability was found by an AI bot, with $200 worth of tokens. Though unlike many other examples, this one was actually fairly deep, and it wasn't low-hanging fruit that illustrated poor code or network security hygiene.

#10378. Review: Amazon’s Alexa Tablet 8 is a Fire Tablet with Google Play for $150 – what’s not to love?

Amazon shocked everyone in October when it launched its new Alexa Tablet series with Google Play support built-in, addressing the biggest shortcoming of its older Fire Tablets. For Black Friday 2026, the Alexa Tablet 8 is a mere $150 and, with Google Play on board, this feels like a Fire Tablet that’s all grown up.

The Alexa Tablet 8 is one of three new Amazon tablets that launched in October with Google Play on board, and it’s also the one I was personally the most excited about. I love a good tiny Android tablet, but there just aren’t that many out there – and some of the ones we do have are exorbitantly expensive.

Right up there with Amazon’s long-running reputation in the tablet space, Alexa Tablet 8 is very much an affordable device, and that’s reflected in its fairly meager specs:

Yet, I didn’t find myself put off by the device.

I specifically asked Amazon for a review unit of the absolute base model – 4GB of RAM and lockscreen ads – and I can say with confidence it’s Not Bad at the end of the day.

The display is objectively not good, but it’s not really trying to be flashy either. The panel gets bright enough for most indoor use and, for general browsing or watching videos around the house or even on a flight, it gets the job done. If anything, my biggest complaint on the display was just how smudgey it gets with fingerprints.

Hardware is a strong suit. The Alexa Tablet 8 has an aluminum build that comes in this gorgeous teal color as well as a softer “Rosette” pink (or “Graphite,” if you want to keep it boring). It’s thin and light and slips into a bag easily, and even fit into the back pocket of my jeans. Buttons are clicky and aspects such as the speakers actually do well enough – like the display, the speakers aren’t good, but they’re not bad by any stretch either.

Amazon has a folio cover available that’s simple but nice, with a fabric finish that’s grippy. It’s an extra $40, though, which feels like a bit much for a super-affordable Android slate.

The biggest question was around the software experience, and this is where this new effort really shines.

Alexa Tablet 8 ships with Android 16 out of the box, not FireOS or whatever Amazon was calling its forked experience on Fire Tablets (I genuinely refuse to go double-check because it’s a memory I’m thrilled to never have to revisit). It’s a pretty “stock” experience, with Amazon leaving aspects such as the notification shade/quick settings alone, and even the Settings menu feels pretty clean.

That’s not to say nothing has changed. The homescreen has a fairly standard layout , but with these neat little corner widgets – one a “dynamic” widget with scrolling content (mostly ads) and the other a shortcut for Alexa search. Amazon is pushing Alexa as a voice assistant pretty heavily on these tablets, but I’m happy to report it’s pretty easy to ignore if you choose. These tablets will be great additions to your Alexa ecosystem if you’re invested, but I’m simply not.

The biggest change you’ll find is the lockscreen, where Amazon’s base models all come with lockscreen ads. This saves you about $20 on the hardware, and it’s not that bad of an experience. That said, I’d absolutely spend the extra $20 at purchase to go without them, especially seeing as this blocks the ability to set a lockscreen wallpaper at all. It doesn’t cost anything more to remove them later, though, with a $20 charge through your Amazon account removing ads.

But what’s important here is that, for the fire time, Amazon is admitting defeat and installing the Google Play Store.

Alexa Tablet 8, and the rest of the lineup, has Google Play out of the box and access to any app you want. This means that you can, if you choose, fully ignore Amazon’s own services and do whatever you want. The smallest of examples: While Amazon’s built-in keyboard is fine, it’s wonderful that I don’t have to jump through hoops to switch over to Gboard. Fire Tablets were always an enticing value with a big catch, but Amazon’s change in this regard truly makes Alexa Tablets an attractive option for everyone – not just those willing to put up with the restriction or put in the work to hack Google apps on board.

Performance, despite the meager specs, is not awful. MediaTek’s low-end chip has just enough horsepower for general use, and 4GB of RAM isn’t as detrimental as I would have expected. It’s not fast by any means, but you could do a lot worse. There are 4GB Android tablets I’ve literally decided not to review because I couldn’t use them past the setup process, so the fact that Amazon is delivering a completely usable experience is a win in my book, even more so that I don’t actually mind using it. There’s a caveat to that, of course, and it’s that you shouldn’t expect to do a ton on this base model. For light browsing, reading Kindle books (or whatever other reading app since, you know, Google Play), or watching videos, this is great. For games, you’ll want to look elsewhere (and expect to spend more).

Alexa Tablet 8 isn’t some shining example of a perfect budget tablet, but I think it really nails the fundamentals. At its usual $230~ starting price, it’s a plenty-good deal for those in the market for an affordable Android slate, but the Black Friday pricing of just $150 is all the better – I just wish the model with 8GB of RAM was also on sale.

Amazon’s pivot to Alexa Tablets is great – though I hope the company knows full well that everyone is still going to call these Fire Tablets or Kindles or whatever combination of brands that spring to mind. But that reputation might work in Amazon’s favor at the end of the day. While there’s a huge portion of the audience that’s been left frustrated for years at the lack of Play Store support on Fire Tablets, they’ve still been a market success for the most part, so this shift is just a win for everyone. Users get a better experience, Amazon gets to shuffle its priorities away from a dead app store, and the market as a whole gets an affordable piece of hardware that doesn’t suck.

#10376. Best Ecommerce Platforms for Developers: Six Options and the Layers Each Leaves You

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

Picking an ecommerce platform is really a decision about which layers you want to own: the storefront, the catalog and order logic, the payment flow, and the hosting underneath. Every platform below draws that line in a different place. If you have already settled the front-end question with a comparison like SitePoint's Astro vs Next.js guide, the next question is what sits behind it. Prices are from each vendor's pricing page as of November 2026.

Shopify is the fully managed option. Basic costs $29 a month with card rates from 2.9% plus 30 cents, and a 2% fee applies to orders processed through a third-party gateway on that plan. Developers get two routes: Liquid themes, or Hydrogen, Shopify's headless toolkit, where your framework owns routing and rendering and Hydrogen supplies the Storefront API client and cart. Templates exist for React Router on Oxygen and for Next.js on Vercel. Plus starts at $2,300 a month.

Whop is a business platform for taking payments and sending payouts, and it sits under a storefront you have already built rather than replacing it. The Checkout element runs in the browser with no server code and no API key on the page. You pass your own order ID as metadata, Whop copies it onto the payment, and your server fulfills from a signed webhook that follows the Standard Webhooks spec. Domestic cards cost 2.7% plus 30 cents with no monthly fee, buyers see payment methods chosen by their location across 195 countries, and the same balance pays out in over 200 countries, including to affiliates or sellers you onboard. Whop's own guide shows how to integrate a checkout API into a Next.js storefront end to end. The trade-off is that there is no catalog or shipping engine; you collect address fields at checkout and trigger fulfillment from the webhook.

WooCommerce is a free plugin on WordPress you host yourself, so you own updates, security, and performance. Its payment gateway, WooPayments, charges US merchants 2.9% plus 30 cents per card transaction, 1.5% more for international cards, 2% for currency conversion, and $25 per dispute. Everything is PHP hooks and filters, which makes it the natural pick for agencies already living in WordPress and the wrong pick for anyone who does not want to run a server.

BigCommerce hosts the back end and lets you bring any front end. Core is $29 a month billed annually, capped at $30,000 in trailing annual sales, and plans auto-upgrade as volume grows. There are no platform fees when you use an embedded payment provider, and a 2% fee on Core if you use another one. Catalyst, its reference storefront, is built on Next.js, React Server Components, and the Storefront GraphQL API, and deploys to Vercel.

Medusa is an open-source commerce framework in Node.js and TypeScript, with an MIT-licensed core and a commercial enterprise edition. SitePoint covered it when it was still a young Shopify alternative; it now has over 37,000 GitHub stars. Self-hosting is free. Medusa Cloud starts at $29 a month for development, $99 for launch, and $299 for scale, with no percentage of sales, and the company says you can export your data and self-host at any point.

Saleor is a GraphQL-native, API-only commerce platform written in Python and released under the BSD-3 license. Self-hosting is free. Saleor Cloud starts at $1,599 a month for up to $200,000 in monthly sales, with 0.8% on orders above that, and $3,999 a month at the next tier. It is built for teams with a dedicated engineering function and a large catalog, which is exactly who should pay that price.

Own only the storefront, and Shopify or BigCommerce will carry the rest. Own everything, and Medusa or Saleor give you a back end you can read. Already on WordPress, and WooCommerce is the obvious fit. Already have a storefront in Next.js or Astro and only need the money to move, and Whop is the shortest path. Whichever you pick, the checkout is where disputes start, so log what SitePoint's friendly fraud guide for developers recommends from day one.

#10375. Google Pixel officially re-releases App Lock in beta, Samsung Galaxy gets it too

It’s been a wild ride for Google Pixel’s App Lock feature, but the company is officially re-releasing the feature today, right alongside its debut on Samsung Galaxy devices.

The feature is still only in Android 17 QPR3 Beta 3 for Google Pixel devices, an update that should be released to the stable track in early 2027.

So some Samsung phones will get it before Pixel phones 🤣

You can still access App Lock by long-pressing on an app icon and adding it to the app lock list.

In a post on Reddit, Google’s Community Engagement Manager details that App Lock on Google Pixel was removed for improvements before getting a full release:

…the product team decided to temporarily pull back the feature from the QPR2 Beta to allow for additional polish, thorough testing, and robust integration across all system surfaces.

That’s why Samsung’s One UI 9.5 beta release today also brings support for App Lock. The feature is built into Android 17 QPR2, seemingly the release that One UI 9.5 is based on, but Google Pixel devices aren’t getting it until QPR3. As far as release timelines go, this means that Samsung’s Galaxy S27 series will probably ship with this feature out of the box, where Pixel phones won’t get it until around March or so. One UI 9.5 won’t roll out for older Galaxy devices until May, if One UI 8.5 serves as an example.

#10374. What is Pathways? Single-controller training for large models

Sorry, your browser doesn't support playback for this video

Today, we announce the General Availability of Pathways on Google Cloud Platform. In this post, you will learn about Pathways, the distributed system Google built to train its own large models, including Gemini. We discuss how Pathways addresses the coordination challenge by introducing one client that describes the whole computation as a graph, and how the distributed runtime places it, moves the tensors, and keeps the accelerators busy.

Most large-model training runs as Single Program, Multiple Data (SPMD) programs. Every accelerator runs the same program in lockstep and coordinates through collectives like AllReduce.

It works well when your job is the same program running on every device. It struggles when the job is no longer uniform, like a pipeline whose stages wait on each other or an RL loop where a sampler, a reward model, and a trainer are different programs that have to overlap.

Large runs also have to survive hardware failures, which interrupted Gemini 2.5 training multiple times per hour. In SPMD, one lost machine stops every process and sends the run back to its last checkpoint. In each case, the challenge is coordination. You have to decide what runs where and in what order, how tensors move between devices, and how the job keeps going when a machine drops out.

In this post of the series “Pathways on Google Cloud”, you will learn about Pathways, the distributed system Google built to train its own large models, including Gemini. The post discusses how Pathways addresses the coordination challenge by introducing one client that describes the whole computation as a graph, and how the distributed runtime places it, moves the tensors, and keeps the accelerators busy.

By the end, you'll know where SPMD is suboptimal, what single-controller execution changes, and how Pathways coordinates a whole training job across a cluster.

If you train PyTorch models, you almost certainly use Distributed Data Parallel (DDP) or Fully Sharded Data Parallel (FSDP). DDP runs a copy of your script on every accelerator and averages gradients with a collective operation such as AllReduce. FSDP shards parameters, gradients, and optimizer states across workers so that the model fits at all.

Sorry, your browser doesn't support playback for this video

DDP and FSDP both follow the Single Program, Multiple Data (SPMD) execution model. Every process runs the same copy of your script, owns its accelerators for the whole run, and moves in lockstep with the others, because the only way they coordinate is through collectives like AllReduce. Inside one tightly connected group of accelerators those collectives run over a fast interconnect and are cheap. Scale the job across groups and the same collectives cross the datacenter network, which is far slower. On a TPU v5p, for example, each chip gets 1,200 GB/s of bidirectional chip-to-chip bandwidth inside its group, but only 50 Gb/s (gigabits, about 6 GB/s) to the datacenter network which is roughly two orders of magnitude less.

And because every step blocks on its collectives, that communication lands directly in step time. The more groups you span, the larger the share of each step spent moving data, so throughput grows slower than the number of accelerators you add.

As long as your training job is one program that fills the hardware you give it, this execution model fits well. You write your model as if there were one device, and the collectives do the rest. But fitting a model into memory is only part of the problem. Keeping every accelerator busy is the harder part, and this is where the SPMD model starts to show its limits.

Take pipeline parallelism as an example. You split a model into blocks across devices, and each device now runs different code over smaller micro-batches. Activations flow forward, activation gradients flow back, and while device 0 works on the first micro-batch, devices 1 and 2 wait. Apart from the idle accelerators you keep paying for, this drops utilization and throughput, even though every device has work in the steady state.

Sorry, your browser doesn't support playback for this video

Another example is RL post-training for an LLM. You have a sampler that generates rollouts, a reward model that scores them, and a trainer that updates the policy and sends the new weights back to the sampler. These are three different programs that should overlap; otherwise the sampler is idle while the trainer runs, and the trainer is idle while the sampler generates.

Sorry, your browser doesn't support playback for this video

In both scenarios, the job is now several different programs running on different devices, and their dependencies can no longer be managed through a single collective. You now have to coordinate which devices share which tensors, which processes run while others are still finishing, and how early to enqueue the next buffer so an accelerator never waits for input. Because each worker only has visibility into its local devices and data, no individual process can manage all of these dependencies itself. That has several consequences.

First, because each process owns its accelerators for the whole run and every collective blocks until all devices finish, one failed machine prevents the collective from completing and stops the entire job, forcing a restart from the last checkpoint. You cannot add devices mid-run, and you cannot give devices back.

Second, because each process holds its accelerators from start to finish, capacity can't move to where it's needed. Within one job, a stage that needs fewer chips than another still keeps the extra ones. They sit idle, you keep paying for them, and no other part of the job can use them until the run ends. Across a team, the same happens at cluster scale. Every job owns its accelerators for as long as it runs, so sharing a cluster means splitting it into per-job allocations, and each new job brings up its own processes on its own hosts.

Finally, the development process itself gets harder. On one host you iterate fast because you run the script, drop into a Python shell, set a breakpoint, and debug. This is not true once the job spans many hosts. You need a launcher to push your script to every host, you hit coordination bugs that never showed up on one machine, and there's no single process to break into. When something hangs, each worker is stuck at a collective waiting for the others, with no one place that shows the whole job.

So the common challenge here is the coordination itself. As soon as the job stops being one program in lockstep, you have to decide what runs where, in what order, and how tensors get from one device to the next.

One way to describe this is as a graph, where each block is a node, each tensor that moves between blocks is an edge, and the devices are where the nodes are placed. You can wire that graph up by hand in SPMD, one process at a time. But what if a single client declared that graph, including what each block does, what it needs, and where it runs, and a distributed runtime handled the transfers, dependencies, and ordering along its edges?

Sorry, your browser doesn't support playback for this video

That is the idea behind Pathways, the distributed system Google built to train its own largest models, including Gemini. Pathways runs JAX programs on TPUs and it is available on Google Cloud.

With Pathways, you define the end-to-end computation from a single Python client, without writing custom coordination code, and a distributed runtime executes it. That client is decoupled from the hardware. It runs on an ordinary CPU machine and you write your program against virtual devices; a resource manager maps them onto real chips, and a worker on each accelerator host runs the compiled programs it receives.

Sorry, your browser doesn't support playback for this video

Because your code refers to virtual devices rather than physical ones, the runtime is flexible to allow users to change the devices used for a given computation. This allows you to stay in control while the runtime handles the mapping to hardware. In an RL loop, for example, the trainer and the samplers rarely want the same number of chips, so you can give the sampler stage more chips by asking for more virtual devices.

Also, the same capability helps when a host fails or the hardware isn't ready yet. If a host dies, the resource manager remaps the work onto healthy chips and the run keeps going instead of relaunching the whole job, which is the basis for elastic training. And because the program targets virtual devices, compilation can start before the physical chips are provisioned, so setup overlaps with provisioning instead of waiting on it.

The resource manager owning the chips also means one Pathways cluster can serve several clients, so researchers on a team can share its capacity. With the Shared Pathways Service, the cluster stays up as a long running service, which lets you develop interactively on TPUs hosted on GKE. Each client connects on demand from a laptop or VM, gets its own slices (groups of tightly connected TPU chips) from the shared pool, and hands them back when it disconnects. When every chip is busy, the next workload waits until capacity frees up.

Take the three-block model from Figure 2. Block A runs on one group of devices, block B on a second, block C on a third. You declare what each block computes, its target devices, and where its outputs go. Pathways expresses those dependencies as a distributed dataflow graph, in which each block is a compiled SPMD program and each edge is a tensor the runtime has to deliver.

Sorry, your browser doesn't support playback for this video

In a single SPMD program, the framework handles the collectives that keep the devices in sync within a group, with or without Pathways, and your code doesn't change. The difference shows up once the job runs several programs on different groups (Multiple Programs, Multiple Data, MPMD), like these three blocks or the sampler, reward model, and trainer of an RL loop. Then no single process sees both sides (the same limit from before, where each worker only knows its own devices), so getting a tensor from block A to block B is a hand-off you have to coordinate yourself.

Pathways closes that gap. You declare the dependency once, and the runtime moves and reshards the tensor for you, over the fast interconnect within a group and over the datacenter network between groups.

To keep the accelerators busy, Pathways prepares launches ahead of time with futures and parallel asynchronous dispatch. While block A is still computing, block B can't run yet, but its input shapes and dtypes are already known, so the runtime allocates B's buffers and schedules the transfer before A's output even exists. Host-side setup for every block happens up front, and each block launches when its inputs arrive.

Sorry, your browser doesn't support playback for this video

This is also what keeps the single client from becoming a bottleneck. Because dispatch is asynchronous, the coordination work overlaps with the compute already running on the chips, so the client stays off the critical path instead of stalling the accelerators between steps.

In Pathways, the runtime decides execution order when a group has more than one operation ready. In pipelined training, B's devices often hold both the forward pass for an upcoming micro-batch and the backward pass for an earlier one. If each device picked on its own, their collectives would conflict and the group would stall.

Pathways has a central scheduler that puts every program's operations into one consistent order, so operations that must communicate start together instead of deadlocking.

Sorry, your browser doesn't support playback for this video

And because it's still one client, the development loop we mentioned before comes back. It's the same single Python process whether you drive one host or thousands of hosts, so you can drop into a shell, set a breakpoint, and inspect the run as it goes. When something stalls, there's one place that knows what's queued on every device and which dependency hasn't been met, instead of a hang spread across separate processes.

Put together, Pathways separates model building from execution. You choose how the model partitions across hardware using the client. And the runtime handles the transfers, ordering, and scheduling that keep accelerators busy, across the whole cluster rather than just your slice of it.

Described this way, Pathways can start to sound like an orchestrator you already know. If scheduling work, moving tensors, and keeping a whole cluster busy brings Ray to mind, it should. But the two work at different layers.

Ray is an open-source framework for orchestrating Python-native AI workloads across large clusters. It provisions the cluster, schedules your Python code as tasks and actors, and launches your workload across hosts, holding data loading, training, serving, and tuning under one control plane. To Ray, an accelerator is a resource to schedule.

What Ray doesn't change is how your training runs on the chips. The job inside each worker still runs as SPMD. The framework inserts the collectives, and each process sees only its own devices. Ray places and starts that job but it doesn't turn it into a single program that drives the whole cluster.

That is the layer Pathways works at. It changes the execution model underneath, so one client coordinates every chip. Because they sit at different layers, the two are complementary. In principle, Ray can orchestrate the cluster and the pipeline while Pathways handles how the training step executes.

So concretely, Ray's job is about orchestration while Pathways' job is about execution. With Ray, you tie your data, training, serving, and tuning together and spread heterogeneous work across accelerators and clouds. With Pathways, you run one model across many groups of chips, express patterns beyond SPMD, and recover from a failed host instead of relaunching the whole job.

When a training job stops being one uniform program, the challenge becomes coordinating the dependencies between stages, the tensors that cross the network, and the ordering that keeps every accelerator fed.

SPMD requires you to address these dependencies. With Pathways, you describe the computation once, from a single client, as a graph of blocks and the tensors between them. And you have a runtime that places the blocks on virtual devices, moves and reshards the tensors, orders the work across the cluster, and remaps around a failed host.

With Pathways, model building and execution are separated and you reason about the whole cluster instead of one slice of it.

A huge capacity upgrade with fast speeds for gaming and productivity
