#10326. Announcing Long-Context Training on TPUs with MaxText Context Parallelism

Long-context training — including pretraining and post-training, has become a foundation of frontier AI models. Longer native sequence lengths give large language models (LLMs) the working memory to read a whole code repository or ingest hours of video, and reason through multi-step agentic tasks. In three years, context windows have grown from 128K–200K tokens to 1M–2M, and models trained toward 10M tokens are arriving. Training at those lengths, not just serving them, is where the system limits show up.

In particular, efficient training at multi-million-token sequence lengths runs into the following bottlenecks:

Today, we are excited to announce long-context training support in MaxText, Google Cloud's open-source JAX training framework. MaxText enables this by supporting four context-parallel strategies on Cloud TPU: All-gather, A2A (DeepSpeed Ulysses), P2P (Ring Attention), and the A2A + P2P hybrid (Unified Sequence Parallelism, or USP), combined with load balancing and sequence packing. All four use the Tokamax Splash Attention kernel. Combined with the 3D inter-chip interconnect (ICI) ( supported on TPUs such as TPU 5p or TPU 7 (Ironwood) ), this lets one sequence span an entire slice while communication overlaps compute and memory per chip stays flat. In our benchmarks, Llama 3.1 8B (25 head dim, 16 query heads) demonstrates exceptional long-context efficiency, achieving 70% Model FLOPs Utilization (MFU) on TPU 5p for sequence lengths up to 4 million tokens. Across both TPU 5p and TPU 7, context can be seamlessly extended to 14 million tokens—with TPU 7 consistently maintaining 50% MFU across all sequence scales below 6 million tokens.

This functionality is available today in MaxText v0.2.4(doc).

Context parallelism splits a long sequence across chips, so no chip holds the activations of the whole sequence. Context parallelism can be combined with data and tensor parallelism, and the smallest context-parallel size that fits your sequence in HBM is usually the right one. This also holds for sparser attention variants which cut attention compute but not the activations of every other layer.

Attention is the one global operation where every token needs the keys and values of every preceding token, so each context parallel strategy has to bring those keys and values back together. Each makes a different tradeoff between memory (how much KV a chip holds), communication (how much moves per layer, and whether it overlaps compute), and model shape (how many attention heads to split).

All-gather: Each chip keeps its chunk of the sequence and, at every layer, gathers the keys and values from every other chip. It is the simplest option. However, because causal masking causes later tokens to attend to larger prefixes, All-gather requires balancing compute load across devices.

Sorry, your browser doesn't support playback for this video

All-to-All (Ulysses): Gathering keys and values makes every chip hold the whole sequence, so attention memory never shrinks no matter how many chips you add. All-to-All changes what each chip owns instead. Attention is independent across heads but not across tokens, so the head axis is free to shard and the token axis is not. An All-to-All before attention rotates the sharding from one onto the other: each chip gives up the heads it is not responsible for and receives the tokens it was missing for the heads it keeps, ending up with the full sequence for a few heads instead of a few tokens for all heads. This is also why Ulysses does not need token load balancing. Attention then runs as ordinary single-chip attention with standard kernels, and a second All-to-All rotates the output back. KV per chip shrinks by the context-parallel factor, but you cannot split across more chips than the model has heads — and tensor parallelism shards that same dimension, so TP × CP is capped by the head count (under GQA, by the KV heads: eight for Llama 3 8B). The all-to-all itself, though, does not overlap with compute — attention cannot start on a partial sequence, so every chip waits for data from every other chip before any of them begins.

Sorry, your browser doesn't support playback for this video

P2P (ring attention): Ulysses' ceiling is the head count; ring attention removes it by never leaving the token axis. Keys and values never materialize in full. KV chunks travel around a ring of chips one hop at a time, neighbor to neighbor. Each chip attends its queries to the chunk it holds while the next one is in flight, and merges the partial results as it goes. A chip holds its own chunk and one visiting chunk, never the full sequence, and the communication overlaps compute. However, dividing attention computation into smaller chunks can reduce local kernel efficiency, and unlike All-to-All Ulysses, ring attention requires explicit load balancing.

Sorry, your browser doesn't support playback for this video

A2A + P2P (USP): A2A + P2P (USP): Neither strategy scales on its own — All-to-All stops at the head count, and a long ring accumulates hops while thinning the overlap that makes it work. Unified sequence parallelism composes them on two axes of the chip mesh, so the context-parallel degree becomes the product of the two: All-to-All along one axis, sized at or below the KV-head count, and a ring along the other to extend past it. Because each All-to-All group has already pooled its tokens, a ring step now carries a chunk larger by the All-to-All factor, and the ring is correspondingly shorter — more compute per hop to hide the transfer behind, and fewer hops to accumulate latency and causal imbalance. On Cloud TPU the two map onto two axes of the slice's chip mesh.

Sorry, your browser doesn't support playback for this video

These strategies cover the attention variants in today's major models, from multi-head and grouped-query attention to DeepSeek-style multi-head latent attention.

Splitting the sequence only pays off if the attention compute on each chip stays efficient. All four strategies run on Splash Attention, the block-sparse flash attention kernel from Tokamax, Google's open-source library of TPU kernels. It skips fully masked blocks and handles packed sequences without padding. P2P calls it once per ring step, on the local queries and the KV chunk that has just arrived, while the transfer for the next step runs underneath, in the forward and backward pass alike. Each chip does the same fused, block-level work it would do without context parallelism, with the communication hidden behind it.

P2P (Ring Attention) is the primary workhorse for very long-context training, whereas All-Gather is the standard baseline for most other cases where the KV can fit. These are the primary CP strategies to use in practice. We compare how far each can push context length on a fixed 64-chip TPU 5p slice with Llama 3.1 8B (256 head dim, 16 query heads) , and measure their MFUs. In ideal settings where both strategies can have large enough chunks (S/CP) and local batch size - the two major variables driving MFU on TPU 5p, we observe that P2P can outperform All-Gather almost every context length from 64K to 14M tokens, where communication becomes mostly hidden with the Ring circular transport schedule.

TPU v7x (Ironwood) takes the same recipe further. With twice the HBM per chip, P2P training of 14M tokens on 64 TPU 7 chips reached 48.7% Model FLOPs Utilization (MFU) compared to 37.1% on TPU 5p. We observed that ring attention performs better than other context parallelism strategies -- e.g. Ulysses and USP –- on TPU 7 chips.

The choice of context parallelism strategy depends mostly on whether the gathered KV fits in HBM, and on how many KV heads the model has:

For all CP strategies, when you run low on memory: Offload before you recompute. MaxText can move saved activations, optimizer states, and parameters to host memory tensor by tensor. At long context lengths, these transfers are hidden behind compute, making offloading effectively free. Offload attention outputs first, followed by optimizer states, layer inputs, parameters, and finally remat as a last resort. MFU stays flat through the offload steps while all-gather falls off and eventually stops fitting. For more details, see the remat tuning guide.

Looking ahead, we see two natural extensions. The first is broader model coverage. Models that mix sliding-window and global layers, such as Gemma, would run the window layers with a short exchange between neighboring chips while the global layers use the ring. We also plan to support linear attention mechanisms such as Gated Delta Net (GDN) and Kimi Delta Attention (KDA). The second is performance, with further tuning of the Tokamax kernels and the P2P backward pass to narrow the remaining gap to all-gather at short context, and recipes by model and context length for TPU 5p and 7 chips.

Context parallelism is available today in MaxText v0.2.4 on PyPI, the corresponding Docker images and on GitHub main. Sample recipes are available here for TPU 7 and TPU 5p. Pick a strategy with context_parallel_strategy (ring, ulysses, or usp), set the context-parallel size, and keep the rest of your run the same. The sharding guide has the flags and constraints for each strategy, and the release notes link to the pull requests behind this work. We can't wait to see what you train.

Intel has reduced its workforce by approximately 27,800 employees under Lip-Bu Tan, based on the company's reported headcount figures, but this does not seem to be enough. The company has reportedly initiated another round of layoffs, this time across its global information technology (IT) organization, as its chief executive continues to reduce expenses and simplify the company's operations, reports Business Insider.

The latest cuts in Intel's IT organization reportedly began on November 16, although Intel has not disclosed how many employees will lose their jobs, notes TrendForce citing CTech. Some employees claim that the reductions could affect a double-digit percentage of the department's workforce of around 2,607 people at 55 sites in 53 countries, so we are talking about at least 260 people if we are talking about a 10% reduction, or as much as 520 if we are talking about a 20% reduction. One has to keep in mind that these estimates have not been independently verified. Intel has neither confirmed the number of affected employees nor identified the specific teams involved. Instead, the company reiterated to Business Insider that it regularly reviews its workforce to ensure that personnel and financial resources correspond to its strategic priorities.

The latest reductions follow several rounds of layoffs throughout 2026. In July, Intel eliminated positions in its Data Center and AI Group. Between July and October, the company submitted nine Worker Adjustment and Retraining Notification (WARN) notices in California, which covered 222 employees, according to Business Insider. The most recent notice, filed on October 22, concerned 67 permanent job cuts at Intel's Folsom campus (which mostly specializes in R&D, hardware validation, and software development), effective October 31. As of November 17, no additional WARN notices had been reported.

Intel employed 81,100 people as of September 27, 2026, including 76,500 employees at Intel itself and another 4,600 at Mobileye and other subsidiaries. This compares with 85,100 employees at the end of 2025 and 108,900 a year earlier, which means that the company's workforce declined by 4,000 since December, 2025, and 27,800 since December, 2024. respectively.

Intel's workforce reductions began well before Lip-Bu Tan became chief executive in 2025. In 2024, his predecessor Pat Gelsinger announced plans to eliminate more than 15,000 positions, or approximately 15% of the company’s workforce. Tan has continued to reduce expenses, eliminate management layers, and discontinue lower-priority projects as Intel attempts to cut costs and reduce losses/improve profitability.

#10320. How AI Image-to-Image and Image-to-Video Tools Work, and How to Build a Reliable Workflow

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

Creating polished visual content used to require professional equipment, editing skills and hours of manual work. Generative AI has changed that for photographers, designers, marketers and developers who build creative tools, but the results depend heavily on understanding what these systems actually do. Two techniques sit at the center of most workflows: image-to-image generation, which transforms an existing picture, and image-to-video generation, which adds motion to a still.

This article explains how both techniques work, which settings control the outcome, how several kinds of tools differ, and how to assemble a workflow with review steps, including a short code example you can run yourself. It also covers rights, authenticity and the limits of the technology. Feature details come from each vendor’s own pages at the time of writing and change often, so confirm current terms before you plan a production.

Image-to-image resembles text-to-image, but it starts from your picture instead of pure noise. According to the Hugging Face Diffusers image-to-image guide, the initial image is encoded into latent space and noise is added to it. The model then uses the prompt and the noisy latent to predict and remove that noise, and a decoder converts the result back into an image.

A single setting, usually called strength or creativity, decides how much of the original remains. In Diffusers, a higher strength gives the model more freedom, and a value of 1.0 means the starting image is largely ignored. Strength is linked to the number of denoising steps: with 50 inference steps and a strength of 0.8, the pipeline adds noise for 40 steps and then denoises for 40.

The ranges above are rules of thumb, not guarantees. Models and tools respond differently, so test on your own images. The script below, adapted from the Diffusers documentation, sweeps four strength values on one source image so you can compare them side by side.

Running this requires a compatible GPU setup and model downloads, and the checkpoint name and defaults can change between library versions, so check the current documentation.

Plain image-to-image can drift from the layout you wanted. Research such as ControlNet adds spatial control to pretrained text-to-image diffusion models, with conditions such as edges, depth, segmentation and human pose. In practice, this is why many tools offer reference images, sketches, masks and pose guides on top of a prompt: they supply structure that a text prompt cannot describe precisely.

The key advantage is speed of iteration. The main limits are consistency between variations, small details such as hands and text, and the gap between a convincing concept and a usable final asset.

The right tool depends on what you need to control and where the output will be used. The table summarizes what each vendor’s own page states, and “not stated” means the page did not say, not that the feature is absent.

OpenArt’s own page describes an AI image to image generator that accepts JPEG, PNG, WEBP or HEIC files, offers a creativity slider and an optional reference image, and lets you switch between several models. Its FAQ advises uploading only images you own or have rights to use. Adobe’s Firefly page describes generative editing and video tools and states that outputs include Content Credentials, while saying its own models were trained on licensed and public-domain content. These are vendor statements, so read the current terms for the plan you use.

Image-to-video uses a still as the starting point for an animated scene. Most current systems extend diffusion models across time, so that a whole sequence of frames is denoised together. The research paper Video Diffusion Models describes this extension from images to video. The practical consequence is that the first frame strongly shapes what follows, so a clean composition and a clear subject make better clips.

For example, a still of a person walking through a rainy city can be animated with falling rain, reflections and a slow camera move. The motion instruction should describe one clear action and one camera movement rather than a long list.

Kling’s main site announces Kling 4.0 and describes stable dynamic motion with an audio-visual experience, with image-to-video features and an API platform listed. OpenArt’s Kling 4.0 page describes how the model is offered on that platform. According to that page, videos run from 3 to 30 seconds at 720p to 1080p, you can use up to 10 keyframes and up to 15 references, and you can save a character for reuse. The page does not state pricing or generation quotas for the model. Capabilities depend on the platform that provides access and on the settings available there, so check supported inputs, output limits and cost before planning a production.

Generated motion needs checking. Warped limbs, drifting faces, flickering textures and unwanted text are common. A quick review script samples frames and reports basic file properties:

Consider a small business preparing a campaign for a new skincare product. The same steps apply to any visual project.

This process reduces the effort of exploring concepts and makes it easier to test several versions of an advertisement before choosing a direction. It does not remove the need for human review.

They can be, with different priorities. Photographers can explore creative treatments, mood boards and alternative backgrounds without replacing traditional photography. Marketers can develop campaign concepts, create variations of social graphics and test promotional videos. In both cases the value comes from supporting a clear creative objective rather than adopting a tool because it is new.

A technique that transforms an existing image using a prompt, by adding noise to the image and then regenerating it under guidance. A strength or creativity setting controls how much of the original is kept.

Use a lower strength, a reference image or a structure condition such as edges or depth where the tool supports it.

Yes. Image-to-video tools animate a still from a motion description, though complex motion can introduce inconsistencies, so review every clip.

No. Several vendors and platforms offer video generation. Compare supported inputs, length, resolution, cost and licensing against your needs.

It depends on the tool, the plan and the rights you hold in the inputs. Read the current terms and keep records of your sources.

Image-to-image and image-to-video tools give creators a faster way to explore ideas, but they work best when you understand their controls. Strength settings decide how much of the original survives, conditioning methods preserve structure, and the starting frame shapes video. Compare hosted platforms, vendor apps and open-source libraries against your needs, build review and provenance into the workflow, and treat the output as a draft that a person checks before it ships.

The real bottleneck isn't AI chips, but power grids.

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

The AI data center build-out is pushing electricity grids across the globe, with many regions facing demand from projects that would exceed the current peak load that each system could support. According to the International Energy Agency’s (IEA) World Energy Outlook 2026 report, a total of 120GW of capacity is currently available across all types of data centers across the globe, with another 120GW expected to be completed by the end of the decade, 75% of which would be dedicated to artificial intelligence. Aside from that, it’s also tracking 93 large “AI factories” that are either under construction or being expanded via satellite imagery. It estimates that the capacity for these sites grew from under 2GW to 17GW in just two years, and that they’re expected to hit a total of 50GW upon completion, the equivalent of adding a second Great Britain to power demand.

This massive power consumption is one of the reasons for the opposition against their construction in communities, with residents blaming them for increased utility prices. However, the IEA says that its analysis “does not yet show a clear relationship between growth in data center capacity and increases in electricity prices.” It noted that if a data center uses existing capacity and pays for its share of the cost, then it could potentially lead to lower utility bills for all customers. However, if the grid operator is required to upgrade its system to accommodate the large consumer, then it could also lead to increased monthly bills if the costs are spread across all users.

Emissions are another issue that the IEA has raised. Renewable energy dominates data centers’ power purchase agreements at nearly 50%, but actual power generation puts coal- and natural-gas power sources closer to 60%. This is likely due to the need for reliability, with the latter kicking in when the former cannot deliver the power that the data center needs. Aside from that, a few projects are deploying their own power, like Elon Musk’s Colossus 2 in Southaven, Mississippi, with about 180GW planned or under construction in the U.S. So, even though renewable energy is growing to accommodate demand, it’s also expected that coal- and natural-gas emissions from data centers will rise by 70% by 2030.

Investments in AI data centers are expected to hit $32 trillion by 2050, putting the infrastructure spending for the industry larger than railways, electrification, and the internet, combined. Despite that, the IEA says the grid remains a significant bottleneck — renewable power plants take between one and five years to complete, which is around the same time that some massive data center projects take if they do not face an extended moratorium, while transmission works can take three to five times longer. This is probably one of the reasons why some AI hyperscalers are choosing to put jet engines on their sites instead, although these are also facing shortages, especially in turbine blades.

#10315. AI features could be turned off with a simple toggle, Canva proves

AI has taken over everything in the past few years, with AI features bombarding virtually every app or service available. If you’ve ever wanted to just turn off AI features, Canva proves that it could be a simple toggle, if companies actually wanted to give you a choice.

Canva today announced “AI Controls,” a new toggle that completely turns off generative AI features within the editing app.

Generative AI can be quite helpful in working on posters, editing images, and more, but it’s not everyone’s preference. Canva puts this well:

For some people, AI is the most exciting creative tool in a generation, while others prefer bringing their ideas to life without it. We believe the future of creativity should give everyone the freedom to choose their own path, whether that’s creating with AI, without it, or somewhere in between.

To give its users the choice, Canva’s Permissions menu now has a simple toggle for “Enable Generative AI features.” Toggling it off disables features such as Magic Write and Magic Edit. There’s also an option to limit how much AI-generated imagery appears in the Canva library.

AI has its place, but everyone should have a choice like this, especially when it comes to apps and services used for artistry in the same vein as Canva. Yet, the tech industry continues to go in the opposite direction, shoving AI into every corner whether it’s actually useful or not. Canva proves, though, that it doesn’t have to be that way.

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

Considering the current status of the cybersecurity landscape, some may well say that "0-day" has lost its meaning, The latest high-stakes vulnerability pertains to a bug in Windows 11 (and possibly 10), wherein an attacker can partially bypass the lock screen and run software as the logged-in user, and seemingly access their data. If true, the vulnerability means even BitLocker won't help keep users' files protected.

Unsurprisingly, the bug was discovered by Abdelhamid Naceri, the prolific Windows hacker known to the world as Nightmare Eclipse, and is responsible for about a dozen zero-day findings in Microsoft's operating system. The exploit requires physical access to the machine, but that's precisely the scenario that lock screens and/or BitLocker are supposed to protect against.

To trigger the vulnerability, all you need is a keyboard. In the Windows 11 lock screen, click "Forgot my PIN," then hit Win+U to bring up the voice narrator. The window will be hidden behind the lock screen, which, for practical purposes, is just a veneer, but you can interact with it using the keyboard if you hold down the Caps Lock key and use the arrows and Enter.

After some brief navigation through Narrator, you'll actually be in Settings, and then have multiple avenues for further shenanigans. One obvious one is going into Bluetooth and Devices and setting Autoplay to show a USB stick's files and folders, then plugging in your own with your software of choice to run. Additionally, on a personal machine, the user is bound to have administrator permissions, so you can just create your own account.

This bug is quite fresh, so there isn't widespread reporting or testing. The author claims "the vulnerability seems to be extremely consistent in every machine I tested it in." We did encounter a sysadmin on Reddit, danrhodes1987, who claims they've tested it on machines they manage, and sure enough, it works, though only on their InTune-enrolled devices. He says that "you can launch anything you want in the context of the user logged onto the device."

Additionally, they found it possible to invisibly navigate whichever programs the user had open before they locked their screen, which all but guarantees that the victim's entire data and programs will be fully accessible. As a Windows sysadmin friend of mine remarked, Windows' lock screen implementation always felt like a kludge, and he posits this won't be the last hack found for it.

#10311. Proton Pass is up to 60% off for Black Friday—and it does way more than just store your passwords

There’s a familiar interruption that comes with shopping online: you’ve found what you want, you’re ready to check out, and the store asks you to sign in. Was this the account with your usual password? Did you change it last time? Is it easier to reset it again?

Proton Pass is designed to take that friction out of your day. It creates strong, unique passwords, remembers them for you, and fills them in when you log in. But it also handles something a password alone can’t: keeping your real email address out of another retailer’s database.

For Black Friday, Proton Pass Plus and Pass Family are both 60% off their regular monthly pricing with a one-year subscription, while Proton Unlimited is 50% off. Pass Plus starts at $1.99 per month, billed annually, making this a good opportunity to get your passwords — and a few other important parts of your digital life — organized.

The problem with strong passwords isn’t understanding why you need them; it’s keeping track of a different one for every account.

Proton Pass does that work for you. When you sign up for a website, its password generator can create a strong password that’s completely unique. Save it in your vault, and Proton Pass can fill in your credentials when you return.

That’s useful whether you’re creating an account for a single purchase or signing into a service you use every day.

Unique passwords also help limit the fallout from a data breach. If a retailer exposes your login credentials, using a different password elsewhere means those same credentials won’t unlock your other accounts.

With unlimited logins and encrypted vaults that sync across devices, Proton Pass lets you keep those details together as you move between your phone and computer.

Proton Pass is available for practically every platform, from Chrome and Firefox on the web to native macOS, Windows, and Linux apps to, of course, iOS and Android. This makes it trivially easy to access your passwords, passkeys, and API credentials wherever you find yourself.

Black Friday shopping often leaves something behind long after the packages arrive: a steady stream of promotional emails from stores you barely remember visiting.

Proton Pass includes unlimited hide-my-email aliases, giving you an alternative to handing out your real address every time you create an account.

An alias is an extra email address that forwards messages to your normal inbox. You can give it to a shopping website and still receive your order confirmations and delivery updates, without sharing the underlying address you use for everything else.

If that alias starts receiving spam, you can switch it off. Your real email address stays the same, and you don’t have to update all your other accounts.

It’s a practical way to put some distance between your primary inbox and the websites you’re trying for the first time. A separate alias for each service also gives you more control: you can stop messages arriving through one address without disrupting the others.

Passwords are only part of what’s worth keeping in a secure, accessible place.

Proton Pass also stores notes, credit card details, and other confidential information in end-to-end encrypted vaults. That makes it useful for the small, everyday moments when you need something sensitive and don’t want to go looking for it.

When you’re booking a flight, saved passport details let you look up the information you need without digging through a drawer halfway through the reservation.

When you’re shopping online, saved card details mean you don’t have to leave the checkout page to find your wallet.

When you’re traveling, keeping a digital copy of an important document within reach gives you a convenient reference while you’re away from home. It doesn’t replace an original document where one is required, but it can save you from searching through scattered files and messages.

Pass Plus also includes a built-in two-factor authenticator, Dark Web Monitoring, and secure link and vault sharing. Those features give you more ways to manage access to your accounts and share sensitive information when you need to.

Pass Plus is the straightforward choice for one person who wants password generation, autofill, unlimited aliases, and secure storage.

Pass Family includes six Pass Plus accounts and a family admin panel. If several people in your household need a password manager, it’s worth considering rather than setting everyone up with separate subscriptions.

Proton Unlimited bundles Pass with Proton VPN, Proton Mail, Proton Drive, and more, including 500 GB of cloud storage. It’s the option to consider if you also want a VPN, encrypted email, and cloud storage alongside your password manager.

A password manager isn’t the most conspicuous Black Friday purchase, but it’s one you can put to work immediately: the next account you create, the next password you forget, or the next time a checkout asks for details you don’t have handy.

Proton’s offers are available to new users and include a 30-day money-back guarantee.

#10310. Markdown to PDF in 2026: No-Install Methods and the Rendering Problems Nobody Warns You About

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

Converting Markdown to PDF is easy until the document has real content: long code blocks, wide tables, images and non-Latin text are where most tools fall apart.

Pandoc with LaTeX is still the most powerful route, but it needs a large install and trips over Unicode and emoji unless you switch engines and fonts.

Browser-based approaches (print CSS, Chromium-powered converters, in-browser tools) avoid the install and give you HTML-quality rendering, as long as you control page breaks.

A handful of print CSS rules (@page, break-inside: avoid, break-after: avoid) fix the majority of ugly PDFs, whichever tool you use.

Choose the tool by the document: a quick README export, a long technical report and a multilingual document each have a different best option.

I write almost everything in Markdown: READMEs, release notes, client handovers, meeting notes. And at least once a week, someone asks for "the PDF version". Every time, the conversion looks fine on the first page and falls apart somewhere around page three: a code block split in half, a table running off the edge, an emoji replaced by an empty box.

SitePoint covered the classic route back in 2015 with Creating PDFs from Markdown with Pandoc and LaTeX. That approach still works. But a lot has changed since then, and today there are several ways to get a clean PDF without installing a TeX distribution. This article compares them, then goes through the rendering problems you'll actually hit, with a fix for each.

Pandoc is still the Swiss Army knife. The simplest command is:

By default, that goes through LaTeX, which means you need a TeX distribution installed (several gigabytes for a full one). The output is beautiful for prose and math, but LaTeX has strong opinions about layout, and customizing it means writing LaTeX.

Pandoc can also render through an HTML engine instead, which lets you style the PDF with CSS:

Best for: long documents, academic writing, math, automated pipelines.

Watch out for: install size, Unicode handling with the default engine (more on that below).

Render the Markdown to HTML (any previewer, a static site, a GitHub page) and print it to PDF. It's free and already installed everywhere, but you inherit whatever the page looks like on screen: navigation bars, sidebars, and page breaks wherever they happen to fall.

Best for: quick, one-off exports of short documents.

Watch out for: zero control over page breaks unless the page ships print CSS.

Extensions like Markdown PDF for VS Code, or CLI tools like md-to-pdf, render your Markdown to HTML and print it with a headless Chromium:

You get the same rendering quality as a browser, plus a config file for CSS, margins, headers and footers. The trade-off is a Node install and a downloaded Chromium.

Best for: developers who live in their editor or want CI-friendly exports.

Watch out for: the first run downloads a browser, and print CSS is still your job.

The newest option is tools that do the whole conversion inside the browser tab: you paste or drop the .md file, preview the actual pages and download the PDF. The good ones don't upload your file anywhere, which matters for client documents and internal notes.

Best for: people who don't want to install anything, non-developers on your team, private documents.

Watch out for: many online converters do upload your file to a server; check before you paste anything confidential.

Whichever method you pick, the same handful of problems show up as soon as the document is longer than a page. Most of them are fixed with print CSS, which works in Pandoc's HTML engines, Chromium-based tools and any HTML you print from a browser.

A 20-line function cut after line 3, with the rest on the next page, is the most common complaint. The fix is to ask the renderer not to break inside the block:

One caveat: a block longer than a full page has to break. For those, it's better to let it break cleanly than to push it to a new page and leave a huge gap.

On screen, a long line in a code block scrolls horizontally. On paper, there's no scrollbar, so it's simply cut off. Wrap long lines in print:

Tables with many columns overflow the page or get squeezed until every cell is one word per line. Three things help: a smaller font size for tables, a landscape page for the worst offenders, and avoiding breaks in the middle of short tables:

If a table genuinely needs eight columns, consider whether it should be a table in the PDF at all, or an appendix in landscape.

A heading alone at the bottom of a page, with its content on the next one, looks broken. This rule keeps a heading attached to what follows it:

Support for break-after: avoid varies between rendering engines, so test it with yours; when it isn't honored, a manual page break before the heading is the reliable fallback.

Markdown has no syntax for a page break. The usual trick is a raw HTML element that print CSS turns into a break:

Some converters also recognize a comment like , which has the advantage of staying invisible when the same file is rendered on GitHub.

Images are inserted at their natural size, so a 4000-pixel screenshot spills off the page. Cap them:

For blurry images, the problem is usually the opposite: a small screenshot stretched to page width. Export screenshots at 2x if they're going into a PDF.

This one is specific to the LaTeX route. With the default pdflatex engine, Unicode characters outside its supported set produce errors or simply vanish. Switch to XeLaTeX or LuaLaTeX and give it a font that actually contains the characters:

Emoji are harder still in LaTeX and often need a dedicated package. HTML-based renderers handle both far better, because they use the operating system's font fallback just like a web page does.

Internal links ([see setup](#setup)) and external links should stay clickable. HTML-based renderers usually keep both. If yours doesn't, check that headings get IDs (most Markdown parsers add them) and that the PDF engine preserves link annotations.

Put together, these rules make a solid starting point for any HTML-based route:

Save it as print.css and pass it to Pandoc (--css=print.css with an HTML engine), to md-to-pdf through its config, or include it in the HTML you print from the browser.

My rule of thumb after a lot of trial and error:

A quick README or notes export, no install, private content: an in-browser converter. Full disclosure: this need is why I built the free Markdown to PDF converter on AwesomeToolkit, and it's what I use for this case now. It renders real A4 or Letter pages as you type, has an option to keep code blocks and tables whole, adds page numbers and a table of contents, and the conversion happens in the browser, so the file isn't uploaded anywhere.

Documentation in a repo, exported in CI: md-to-pdf or Pandoc with an HTML engine, plus the stylesheet above.

Long reports, math, academic formatting: Pandoc with XeLaTeX, using a font that covers every script in the document.

Something you'll print once and forget: the browser's Print to PDF is fine.

Whatever you choose, the biggest quality jump comes from the print rules, not the tool. Ten lines of CSS turn "it looks fine until page three" into a PDF you can actually send.

Yes. Your browser's Print to PDF works on any rendered Markdown, and in-browser converters do the whole conversion in a tab. Check that the converter processes files locally if the content is private.

The default pdflatex engine has limited Unicode support. Use --pdf-engine=xelatex or lualatex and set a font that contains the characters with -V mainfont= (and -V CJKmainfont= for CJK text).

Markdown has no native syntax for it. Insert

where you want the break, or use the page-break comment your converter supports.

Add pre { break-inside: avoid; } to your print CSS. Blocks longer than a page will still break, which is expected.

With HTML-based renderers, usually yes, both external links and links to headings inside the document. Test internal links in particular, since they depend on headings having IDs.

Unlike some routers that outright lie about supporting WiFi 7, the Archer BE9500 actually has the magical combo that fully enables the power of the latest protocol: a 6 GHz band, along with Multi Link Operation (MLO) support.

The TP-Link Archer BE9500 is a full-featured Wi-Fi 7 router with full 6 GHz and MLO support and a 10 Gbps WAN port for high-speed internet connections.

That grants the BE9500 an entire 9.5 Gbps of combined wireless bandwidth across all three frequencies, broadcast into the airwaves by six beam-forming antennas. For physical connections, there's one 10 Gbps WAN port, along with two 2.5 Gbps connectors for your big workstations or gaming rigs, and an additional two gigabit ports.

As befits a modern router, the Archer BE9500 supports EasyMesh for wiring additional devices, as well as separate guest and internal networks for segregating IoT and other classes of devices. There are four VPN flavors on offer for acting as both a server and a client, including support for the modern WireGuard protocol.

A USB port for connecting storage media, parental controls, automatic firmware upgrade, VLAN tagging, and miscellaneous features round out the main features. Get this router while stocks last, for only $89.99 at Best Buy.

#10304. On-Device Speech Recognition and Text-to-Speech in Google AI Edge Gallery

Today, we are introducing on-device Automatic Speech Recognition (ASR) and Text-to-Speech (TTS) capabilities in Google AI Edge Gallery. Voice is often the most natural way to interact with AI on phones and wearables. Recent advances in compact speech architectures now make it possible to run accurate, multilingual ASR and expressive TTS models entirely on device, all within a small memory footprint, with low latency, and completely offline. By pairing these lightweight speech models with on-device small language models (SLMs) like Gemini Nano and Gemma 4, developers can build apps that stream live transcriptions as the user speaks and synthesize natural spoken replies locally.

This release brings together major advancements across both Google’s system speech models and the open-weight ecosystem:

Below, we’ll explore how this local voice pipeline powers the new XR (Extended Reality) Language Teacher showcase, upgrades AI Chat in Google AI Edge Gallery, and how you can build with these APIs and models today.

Learning a language while traveling happens in the real world, often in underground stations, busy markets, or abroad where Wi-Fi and roaming data may not be available. To show how vision, language, and speech models can work together 100% locally on your phone with no network connection needed, we built the XR Language Teacher showcase in Google AI Edge Gallery. Simply point your camera at an unfamiliar object or menu, ask "What is that, and how do I ask for one?", and immediately hear an explanation in your language alongside authentic pronunciation in the local language.

Link to Youtube Video (visible only when JS is disabled)

With inference running locally on a paired phone, the same experience works standalone on a phone and extends naturally to hands-free smart glasses. To see how we adapted Language Teacher for intelligent glasses check out our recent talk at Android Dev Summit: Building Audio-First Experiences for Intelligent Eyewear with Gemini Nano.

Beyond the XR Language Teacher showcase, we also built ASR and TTS directly into Google AI Edge Gallery’s main AI Chat feature, giving on-device SLMs like Gemini Nano and Gemma 4 a natural, offline voice interface while on the go.

Link to Youtube Video (visible only when JS is disabled)

While multimodal models like Gemma 4 can process audio inputs directly, we provide a modular solution for cascading language models with separate, lightweight voice models for latency optimization and flexibility. Depending on your target devices and model requirements, our open-source code on GitHub shows you the optimized and recommended solution for Android using ML Kit, as well as MediaPipe Tasks and LiteRT for cross-platform coverage and open model support.

For developers building for Android devices, ML Kit’s GenAI Speech Recognition and Text-to-Speech APIs provide a standardized, platform-integrated path out of the box. With the underlying speech models managed through AICore and Google’s on-device speech services, developers get production-grade quality without having to manage model downloads or worry about APK size bloat. Your app also benefits automatically from hardware acceleration across NPU and TPU backends (when available) and seamless background model updates.

Integrating ASR and TTS into cross-platform applications usually requires handling low-level audio framing, Voice Activity Detection (VAD), and complex decoding pipelines. MediaPipe Task simplifies this by providing unified, turnkey ASR and TTS APIs across iOS, macOS, Windows, Linux, and Web that abstract away manual pre-processing, audio stream routing, and platform-specific audio management into simple, high-level interfaces:

Under the hood, ML Kit and MediaPipe Tasks are powered by LiteRT, Google’s high-performance on-device AI inference infrastructure. Cross-platform applications requiring even more fine-grained control over speech processing or custom model architectures can also build on it directly.

Speech models often rely on multiple subgraphs and auxiliary assets, such as encoder/decoder subgraphs and tokenizers for ASR, or phonemizer dictionaries, voice embeddings, and vocoders for TTS. All required components for each model are packaged into a single .litertlm file for easy deployment. The app ships with ready-to-use .litertlm models, including Parakeet-TDT-0.6B-v3 (multilingual ASR), PocketTTS 100M (multilingual TTS), Phonon 31M (ultra-lightweight English TTS), and Kokoro-82M (multilingual TTS). Beyond these, the vibrant Hugging Face LiteRT Community with over 10K+ followers offers a growing catalog of open-weight models, like Whisper, Qwen3-ASR and Moonshine for ASR, or Qwen3-TTS and Matcha for TTS. For developers seeking custom implementations or support for additional open-weight voice models, LiteRT provides a rich set of multimodal model examples to help you get started, with more on the way.

Adding a local voice pipeline takes on-device SLMs beyond typing-based chat interfaces, enabling natural, voice-first interactions that work anywhere, completely offline. All the building blocks to try these experiences or integrate them into your own apps are available today:

#10303. At $600, the Pixel 10 is this year’s no-brainer Black Friday smartphone deal

Phones have gotten a lot more expensive over the last year or so, and it’s a trend we’re expecting to get worse before it can get better. If you’re in the market for an upgrade, this year’s Black Friday might be one of the final truly good times to buy a new device, and for just $600, last year’s Pixel 10 should be high on your list.

While buying year-old hardware typically comes with some pretty deep sacrifices between generations, that’s not particularly true with the Pixel 10 this year. From a hardware perspective, nearly everything is either identical or close enough to not particularly matter. Its design and display are all but unchanged, Qi2-ready magnets come on both models, and both devices are built with the same level of battery longevity in mind. And much to the chagrin of many a Pixel die-hard, Tensor G5 and Tensor G6 are practically interchangeable.

So what are you giving up that matters? When the Pixel 11 arrived, software features made up the bulk of this list, but with last week’s Pixel Drop, a lot of the gaps between these two generations have been closed. Gemini Intelligence with Rambler, app automation, and more are in the process of rolling out to existing Pixel 10 users, and new features like Create My Widget arrived on the Pixel 10 and 11 at the exact same time. From a software perspective — Google’s bread and butter, in my eyes — the only big sacrifice comes from the year of software support that’s already complete, leaving you with just six instead of seven.

Realistically, the camera is the one place you’re likely to feel a difference. The Pixel 10 was the first non-Pro device in Google’s Tensor era to ship with a telephoto lens, but it came with some big sacrifices. The main sensor is effectively an A-series lens, and taken together, the entire package might leave something to be desired. It’s far from a bad camera though — I used it as my main shooter through most of its lifecycle — and while the Pixel 11 did get some upgrades here, I think real shutterbugs would be better off looking at Google’s Pro lineup anyway. You’ll also lose out on things like HiLight, though really, are you missing that much?

What makes this deal so good is really its place in the market, not as a direct comparison to the newer Pixel 11. For $600, Google’s competition is going to have a hard time competing with the value the Pixel 10 delivers. Hell, even Google might find it difficult to compete in the coming months; at full price, $600 is what the Pixel 10a goes for now, a decidedly less-impressive device compared to this model. No, this is a flagship smartphone through and through, and for $600, you’re effectively guaranteeing a reliable, powerful smartphone experience from now until 2032 — hopefully enough time for this whole RAM crisis to finally simmer down a bit.

#10297. What Is the Best UI design Tool? 8 Tools Compared by the Job They Do (2026)

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

The ideal UI testing tool is the one that discovers what your users discover first: broken layouts, usability issues that no longer work, and new accessibility issues in all the browsers your users use before a change goes into the repository. In 2026, most frontend teams don't rely on a single tool to do all of that. The best setups involve two layers: a test runner layer that launches the browser and monitors behavior, and a UI testing and review platform that visually checks the outcome and routes changes to the right stakeholders for sign-off.

Even with the right stack, it still relies on what your team needs to catch. Different teams will focus on different points: a QA team trying to automate checkout flows, a design system team defending shared components, and a single developer working on a tight budget will all be somewhere different. This guide is categorized by the job these UI testing tools do, then connects them.

Most "best UI testing tool" lists rank test runners and visual testing platforms against each other as if they were interchangeable. They aren't. UI testing covers four distinct jobs:

Functional testing: Does the UI respond correctly when a user clicks, types, or hovers?

Visual testing: Does the UI still look right across layout, color, typography, and spacing?

Accessibility testing: Can people using keyboards and screen readers use it, and did this change introduce new violations?

Cross-browser and device coverage: Does it work in Safari on mobile, not just Chrome on a developer's laptop?

There are also other levels of UI tests. Unit tests test the logic but don't interact with the UI. End-to-end (E2E) tests represent the full user journey in a browser but are difficult to set up, hard to troubleshoot when the bug is a visual detail, and can't practically test every state. Between the extremes of unit tests and E2E tests, component tests simulate each component's behavior in a real browser using fixed inputs, offering speed closer to unit tests and user-level coverage closer to E2E. Many teams maintain a thin layer of E2E tests for critical journeys, and they have component tests for the dozens of states a component may be in.

These tools are organized by job so you can stack them rather than choose one winner

These platforms sit on top of your test runner. They capture rendered UI, compare it to approved baselines, and give your team a place to review what changed.

Chromatic is a UI testing and review platform made by the Storybook team. It catches visual, interaction, and accessibility bugs in components and pages before they merge, and it gives developers, designers, and product managers one place to review UI changes and sign off. Chromatic's team is the core maintainer of Storybook, which has about 90,000 GitHub stars and 17 million weekly npm downloads, and a Vitest core maintainer on the team built Chromatic's Vitest integration. Chromatic is used by 45 Fortune 100 companies, including GitHub, Shopify, Microsoft, Adobe, the BBC, and monday.com.

Pricing: Free plan with 5,000 snapshots per month (Chrome only), unlimited projects and users. Starter is $179/month for 35,000 snapshots and adds Safari, Firefox, and Edge. Pro is $399/month for 85,000 snapshots. Enterprise is custom, with SSO, SCIM, SLA support, and data retention. TurboSnap snapshots count as 0.2 of a regular snapshot.

Trade-offs: Chromatic is strongest when your UI is built from components. Teams with no component tests can start from Playwright or Cypress E2E tests, but they'll get the most value once components are covered too. Snapshot usage also grows with each browser and viewport you add, so plan coverage before you scale it.

Best for: Design system teams, product teams that want designers and PMs to approve UI changes, React Native teams, and Vitest-first teams that want visual testing without baselines stored in the repo.

Percy is BrowserStack's visual testing platform. It captures screenshots from your existing tests, renders them across browsers and responsive widths, and highlights pixel differences for review.

Strengths: Framework-agnostic SDKs for Playwright, Cypress, Selenium, and Storybook. App Percy covers native mobile apps. The free plan includes 5,000 screenshots per month with unlimited users and projects.

Trade-offs: Percy is page-oriented, and each page rendered in each browser at each width counts as a separate screenshot, so usage climbs quickly with cross-browser coverage. Percy compares feature branches against a base branch, which teams with complex branching should test early. Reviewers see rendered screenshots rather than an inspectable copy of the code under test, so debugging a diff usually means reproducing it locally.

Pricing: Free tier, then paid plans with fixed screenshot allowances and overage.

Best for: Teams already paying for BrowserStack that want visual checks on page-level E2E tests.

Applitools Eyes uses Visual AI to compare screenshots the way a person would, with match levels that can ignore content changes and focus on layout. Its Ultrafast Grid re-renders captured page states across many browser, viewport, and device combinations.

Strengths: Mature AI matching, broad browser and device coverage, Figma and Storybook testing, accessibility testing, and an MCP server. Higher tiers add AI-driven functional testing and plain-English test authoring.

Trade-offs: Applitools is priced and packaged for enterprises. There's a free trial but no free plan, and the Starter plan is $667/month billed annually for 100,000 component checkpoints or 1,000 page checkpoints. Professional and Enterprise are custom-priced.

Best for: Large QA organizations with big cross-browser matrices and budget for a dedicated visual AI platform.

BackstopJS is a free, open-source visual regression testing tool released under the MIT license. You list the URLs, viewports, and CSS selectors to capture in a config file, and BackstopJS renders them in a headless browser through Playwright or Puppeteer, then compares the results against approved reference screenshots. The core workflow runs on three commands: backstop init, backstop test, and backstop approve.

Strengths: Completely free, with no screenshot limits. A built-in browser report shows reference and test images side by side with a visual diff inspector, and changes can be approved from there. Optional Docker rendering keeps screenshots consistent between laptops and CI; Playwright adds Firefox and WebKit, and click, hover, and keypress selectors capture interactive states. JUnit reports plug into existing CI pipelines.

Trade-offs: BackstopJS is self-hosted, so there's no cloud dashboard, shared team review, or pull request status checks out of the box. Reference screenshots are usually committed to your repository, and every page or component state you want to test needs its own URL and scenario in the config. Someone on the team also has to own the setup, CI integration, and maintenance.

Best for: Developers and small teams with no budget who are comfortable running and maintaining their own visual regression setup.

Test runners click, type, and assert. They're free and open source, and every platform above builds on them. On their own, they offer limited visual testing: built-in screenshot assertions store baselines in your repository, rendering can drift between laptops and CI, and designers have no review interface.

Playwright is Microsoft's open-source browser automation framework. It runs tests across Chromium, Firefox, and WebKit, with auto-waiting, trace viewer debugging and code generation, and supports JavaScript, TypeScript, Python, Java, and .NET.

Visual testing: Playwright's toHaveScreenshot assertion compares screenshots against baselines stored in Git. It works, but baselines must be regenerated per operating system, and accepting a change means rerunning with an update flag.

Best for: Teams automating critical user journeys such as sign-up, checkout and login. Pair it with a platform like Chromatic for visual review across real browsers.

Cypress is an open-source testing tool that runs inside the browser, with time-travel debugging that shows the app at each step of a test. It supports both E2E and component testing.

Visual testing: Cypress has no built-in visual comparison and relies on plugins or a platform such as Chromatic, Percy or Applitools. Cypress Cloud, its paid service, adds recording, parallelization, flake detection and add-ons for accessibility and UI coverage.

Best for: Frontend developers who want fast feedback and an interactive test runner while they build.

Storybook is the open source workshop for building UI components in isolation. Each story captures one component state, and those stories double as test cases.

Testing features: Interaction tests with play functions and Testing Library, accessibility checks with the a11y addon, and a Vitest integration that runs stories as component tests.

Visual testing: Storybook doesn't diff screenshots on its own. Chromatic, built by the same team, is its visual testing and review layer.

Best for: Design system and component library teams, and any team practicing component-driven development.

Vitest Browser Mode runs component tests in a real browser instead of a simulated DOM, using Playwright or WebdriverIO under the hood.

Visual testing: Vitest's toMatchScreenshot assertion stores screenshots in the repository. For large projects, this can bloat the repo and baselines drift between machines. Chromatic can take Vitest Browser Mode tests with no code changes and run them in a stable cloud environment instead.

Best for: Teams already writing Vitest tests who want them to check how the UI looks without adopting a new framework.

Start from the problem your team is trying to fix:

"Shared component changes keep breaking other teams' screens." Test at the component level with Storybook or Vitest Browser Mode and Chromatic, so the change shows up on the pull request that caused it.

"Our Playwright tests pass, but the layout still breaks in Safari." Functional assertions don't check appearance. Add a visual testing platform that renders in Safari, such as Chromatic, Percy or Applitools.

"We turned off screenshot tests because of false alarms." Look for deterministic component states and flake controls such as Chromatic's SteadySnap, or AI matching such as Applitools.

"Designers find UI bugs after release." Choose a platform with a review and approval workflow, and make sign-off a required pull request check.

"AI coding agents keep inventing components." Give agents access to your real component library, for example through Chromatic's hosted MCP, and run every agent-built change through the same UI tests as human code.

"We have no budget." Use Playwright or Vitest screenshot assertions, self-host BackstopJS, or start on the free tiers of Chromatic or Percy.

A practical, well-covered setup for a React web app looks like this:

Unit tests in Vitest for logic and utilities.

Storybook stories or Vitest Browser Mode tests for every meaningful component state, with interaction tests on key components.

Chromatic running visual, interaction and accessibility tests on those components in Chrome, Firefox, Safari and Edge on every pull request, with designers assigned as reviewers.

A thin layer of Playwright E2E tests for sign-up, checkout and other critical journeys, also captured in Chromatic so page-level visual changes go through the same review.

This keeps most UI coverage in fast, deterministic component tests, while E2E tests confirm the journeys that matter most still work end to end.

Playwright is the best free tool for E2E UI testing, and Storybook is the best free tool for testing component states. For visual testing, For visual testing, Chromatic and Percy both offer free tiers of around 5,000 snapshots or screenshots per month, and BackstopJS is free to self-host.

UI testing is the broad practice of checking that an interface works for users, including functional, visual and accessibility checks. Visual testing is one part of it: comparing rendered UI against approved baselines to catch unintended changes in layout, color, typography and spacing.

No. Chromatic was built by the Storybook team, but it also runs visual tests from Vitest Browser Mode, Playwright and Cypress tests, and from React Native Storybook on iOS and Android simulators.

Playwright's toHaveScreenshot covers basic visual checks, but baselines live in your repo, rendering can differ between machines, and there's no review interface for designers. Teams with several reviewers or many browsers usually add a platform on top.

Most teams use both. Component tests cover individual UI states quickly and reliably, while a smaller set of E2E tests confirms that critical user journeys work across the whole app.

#10290. A2UI 1.0: Build delightful, fast, reliable, agentic experiences

User interactions with agents today are where computers were in the terminal era: capable, and answering almost everything in text. Users can ask an agent for anything by typing or talking, and what comes back is usually a wall of text, a poor way to compare three products or edit and approve a detailed plan.

Today, we're releasing A2UI 1.0, the first stable version of our open protocol for generative UI. It lets AI agents answer with interactive screens, such as a form to confirm or a chart. Developers can use it now with open-source renderer SDKs for the web, Android, and iOS and agent SDKs for Python, TypeScript, and Dart. Companies such as Atlassian, Alation, Bloomberg, and ByteDance are already building with it.

With A2UI, the agent describes what to show, and every app draws the UI with its own native components. The application receives A2UI messages and each app renders the content securely and at native speed. Users get the same low latency, high trust, and complete native fidelity whether they reach a company's agent in its own app or in a third-party AI assistant that hosts MCP apps, such as Gemini or Claude.

You wake up sick. Getting through the morning takes six apps: book a video visit, move your meetings, tell your team, turn on an out-of-office reply, ask your partner to do school pickup and order soup. Ask a text-only agent instead, and you get a numbered wall of text, then round after round of “which ones?”. A2UI won't handle your sick day, but it gives developers the framework to build an agent that can. That agent could answer with one view of all six apps. Changing your plan is a couple of taps instead of another round of messages, and nothing is sent until you confirm.

Sorry, your browser doesn't support playback for this video

The same agent output works on any screen. Ask an agent to “move dinner with Priya to Friday”, and it can answer with one view that lines up your calendar, open tables from a restaurant booking app and prices from a rideshare app, so the time that works for everyone is obvious. The developer builds that view once with A2UI, and it renders natively on your phone and on your laptop.

Sorry, your browser doesn't support playback for this video

In both examples, A2UI is the framework that simplifies building these experiences across platforms. Developers build the agent and choose the platforms it works with. A2UI gives them the protocol the agent uses to describe each view, a way to combine components from different apps in one view, and renderers that draw it natively on the web, Android, and iOS.

New to A2UI? Start with the A2UI Playground by CopilotKit or check out industry examples.

Atlassian is bringing A2UI to Jira, where teams can assign work items to AI agents. When an agent needs a follow-up from the team to finish a work item, such as a missing detail or a decision, it asks with an interactive card in the work item, and the team answers right there in Jira.

The Alation Intelligence Operating System (AIOS) builds its feeds using A2UI: metric cards, charts, and more. Each chart can show the SQL query and data behind it. Ask Claude for a feed about profitable products, and the feed renders in the conversation using A2UI to render metric cards, charts, and custom components. The same feed with the same information can be viewed directly inside Alation with a nearly identical layout.

ByteDance released an A2UI renderer for Lynx, its cross-platform framework, in June 2026. Lynx keeps streamed screens smooth and ships a default set of components built on its `lynx-ui` library. In the Lynx GenUI Playground, you can watch A2UI messages rendered natively, for example, as a two-day trip planner for Kyoto. Lynx supports A2UI 0.9 today and is adapting to 1.0 with the A2UI team.

Ask Gemini Notebook for a slide deck and it replies with a card that asks two things: presenter slides or a detailed deck, and which topics to focus on. A review card follows with the format, focus, and visual style, which you can edit before it generates the deck. The same cards guide Video Overviews on the web and on mobile.

Organizations can register their own agents that respond with A2UI. Gemini renders those responses using the components in its A2UI component gallery, and supports A2UI 0.9.1 today. The Google Cloud documentation covers registering an agent and hosting one on Cloud Run.

Several other Google teams, including some behind our largest products, are building features with A2UI.

Several other Google teams, including some behind our largest products, are building features with A2UI that haven’t launched yet. We’ll share more as they ship.

A2UI and MCP Apps work together. Alation already uses both to show its A2UI feeds in Claude. Our June post, A2UI + MCP Apps, covers three ways to combine them, and the A2UI in MCP Apps guide shows how to run A2UI screens inside an MCP App.

Jetpack Compose support for 1.0 is coming soon. Agents in Gemini Enterprise can already use A2UI, and we're working on upgrading Gemini Enterprise to A2UI 1.0. The A2UI roadmap covers the rest, from design-system primitives that render natively on every platform to official SDK support for decision models like Jev.

We’ve collaborated with partners who have built A2UI 1.0 demos across different industries to give you inspiration on how to adapt A2UI to your use cases. Explore what they’ve built:

Multi-Turn Agentic Reinforcement Learning with Tunix and Gemma 4 on Google TPUs

Distributed LLM Training Across Metros: Streaming DiLoCo in MaxText

Securing Your Agents: A Hands-On Guide to Agent Anomaly Detection

#10289. Build Live Video Chat With WebRTC: From First Call to Production Debugging

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

Adding real-time video to a web app used to mean plugins, proprietary SDKs and a lot of money. Today every modern browser ships with WebRTC, a set of APIs that lets two browsers exchange audio, video and data directly. The catch is that WebRTC gives you building blocks, not a finished feature. This guide walks through those blocks in the order you actually need them, with the exact API calls you need, and then covers the production issues that tutorials usually skip.

A browser-to-browser call has four moving parts:

Start with the local preview. Browsers only allow camera access on HTTPS or localhost, so serve your page accordingly.

1.     Request a stream with navigator.mediaDevices.getUserMedia(), passing video constraints (an ideal width of 1280 and height of 720 is a sensible default) and audio constraints with echoCancellation and noiseSuppression enabled.

2.     Assign the returned MediaStream to the srcObject property of your local video element.

3.     Keep a reference to the stream, because you will add its tracks to the peer connection later.

Set the muted and playsinline attributes on the local video element. Without muted users hear themselves, and without playsinline iOS Safari opens the video full screen.

The peers need a way to find each other. A tiny Node.js server using the ws package is enough for development. Its whole job is to relay messages between the peers in a room:

1.     Create a WebSocket server (for example with the ws package) listening on its own port.

2.     Keep a map of room names to the set of sockets in each room.

3.     When a client sends a join message, store the room name on the socket and add it to that room's set.

4.     For every other message type, forward the raw message to the other sockets in the same room, and never echo it back to the sender.

5.     On disconnect, remove the socket from its room so stale peers do not receive messages.

In production you would add authentication, limit room size to two for one-to-one calls, and validate message types, but the shape stays the same.

Both sides create an RTCPeerConnection, add their local tracks and listen for the remote ones:

1.     Create a new RTCPeerConnection with an iceServers list that contains at least one STUN server.

2.     Loop over the local stream's tracks and call addTrack() for each one.

3.     In the ontrack handler, attach the first remote stream to your remote video element.

4.     In the onicecandidate handler, send every new candidate to the other peer through your signalling channel as a candidate message.

One peer creates an offer, the other answers, and both apply each other's descriptions. Candidates arrive asynchronously in the background.

1.     The caller runs createOffer(), applies it with setLocalDescription() and sends the description as an offer message.

2.     The callee applies the offer with setRemoteDescription(), runs createAnswer(), applies it locally and sends it back as an answer.

3.     The caller applies the answer with setRemoteDescription().

4.     Both sides pass every incoming candidate message to addIceCandidate().

If both peers might start a call at the same time, look up the "perfect negotiation" pattern on MDN. It assigns one side as polite and resolves offer collisions cleanly instead of leaving both peers stuck.

On a home network, STUN alone often works. On corporate Wi-Fi, mobile carriers and symmetric NATs it frequently does not, and the call silently fails to connect. A TURN server relays media when a direct path is impossible. The open-source coturn server is the usual choice.

In practice, your iceServers list should contain your own STUN URL plus a TURN URL with a username and credential, so the browser can fall back to the relay automatically when direct connectivity fails.

Generate short-lived TURN credentials on your backend rather than hard-coding them, and budget for bandwidth: relayed calls pass every frame through your server.

Users deny camera access, unplug headsets and switch from Wi-Fi to 4G mid-call. Catch NotAllowedError and show a clear explanation, listen for devicechange events, and use RTCRtpSender.replaceTrack() to swap cameras without renegotiating the whole call.

Listen to pc.onconnectionstatechange. When the state becomes disconnected, wait a few seconds before giving up, because it often recovers. On failed, call pc.restartIce() to try new network paths.

Use RTCRtpSender.setParameters() to cap the bitrate on weak connections, and read pc.getStats() to monitor packet loss and round-trip time. Showing a small "poor connection" indicator does more for perceived quality than any codec tweak.

Mesh calls, where every participant connects to every other, work for two or three people. Beyond that, upload bandwidth explodes. Group calls need a Selective Forwarding Unit (SFU) such as mediasoup, LiveKit or Janus, which receives each stream once and forwards it to the others.

Tutorials end at a working demo, but the fastest way to understand what production WebRTC looks like is to watch a real call from the inside. Chrome ships an internal diagnostics page that shows every peer connection the browser opens, and it works on any site that uses WebRTC.

1.     Open chrome://webrtc-internals in one tab and keep it open.

2.     In another tab, start a call on your own demo and find its RTCPeerConnection entry.

3.     Expand that entry and look at the ICE candidate pairs: you will see whether the call went direct (host or srflx) or through a TURN relay (relay).

4.     Open the stats graphs for the outbound video stream and watch the bitrate, frame rate and resolution adapt when you throttle the network in DevTools.

5.     Check the negotiated codec and how quickly the connection recovers when you switch Wi-Fi off and back on.

Once you can read your own call, repeat the exercise on a production service. Any browser-based product works, whether a meeting tool or a live video chat that pairs two visitors, because webrtc-internals shows the same data for every site. Expect differences from your demo: more candidate pairs, a relay candidate in the list and a bitrate that adapts faster. Seeing them makes the earlier sections concrete, from why TURN matters to how bandwidth estimation reacts to packet loss.

The one-to-one call above is the core of many products, from telehealth consultations and tutoring to customer support. The engineering challenges grow with the use case: random matching needs queueing and presence on the signalling server, public platforms need moderation and reporting, and global audiences need TURN servers in several regions.

Start with the minimal version, get it working across Chrome, Firefox and Safari on both desktop and mobile, and add TURN before real users arrive. Once the call is reliable, every other feature becomes a much easier problem.

#10283. Google’s 3D emoji has wild Android spec requirements: 10GB of free space, 6GB RAM

Google introduced 3D emoji on Pixel this year, and has just detailed the minimum specifications required to get them on your Android phone.

Noto 3D emoji goes from vector-based 2D emoji to a high-resolution bitmap font. This results in “high-resolution designs” for emoji “with more texture and dimension to characters and icons.” Google has an FAQ section about why the new emoji might look low resolution:

Vector vs. bitmap rendering: Older Android emoji used 2D vector graphics, which scale infinitely to any size without losing resolution. The 3D emoji are bitmap graphics, which are pre-rendered pixel-based images. When these images are scaled up by an app or zoomed in, the pixels stretch, which can cause a softer or lower-resolution appearance.

The Google support article published this week lists the minimum specs your Android device must have to get 3D emoji:

The last two bullet points are rather surprising. In fact, Google even lists instructions for checking how much memory and storage your device has:

In terms of availability, Google says a supported device will “automatically download the 3D emoji when it’s on Wi-Fi and charging.”

At this time, there isn’t a way to force download the emoji.

Google announced 3D emoji in May, with the new design shipping on the Pixel 11 in August. It has since arrived on older Pixel devices.

In terms of OEM availability, Google explicitly says “Samsung devices use a proprietary emoji font and do not receive the 3D font update.” Additionally:

This is also true for third-party messaging apps, such as WhatsApp, who use their own emoji fonts rather than relying on your device’s system font.
