#10224. CSS-Tricks has a new owner!

This is pretty wild, but let’s get right to it because it’s big news:

CSS-Tricks is an independent site once again!

Just to recap: DigitalOcean purchased CSS-Tricks in 2022 from Chris Coyier, who founded the site in 2007. Chris ran it all the way up to that purchase.

Here we are in 2026, and I’m taking independent ownership of the site.

So, wait, who owns the site? That’d be me. (Wow, never imagined I would get to say that!) Of course, I have a small, awesome team to help with the day-to-day management of the site.

That all said, I do envision that some things will change. In fact, I’m planning that they will change in what I hope are very good ways. Maybe a question-answer thing makes sense here since this is very much an evolving discussion.

Yes, I’ve got the keys to the castle, so to speak. And, even after 12 years of working on the site, I’ll admit I’m a little overwhelmed and nervous about that. I mean, it’s an investment for sure, as you can imagine, and I’m making this my full-time job. At least for a while.

I want to make CSS-Tricks both a real community and a place that’s inclusive of everyone, no matter where they are in their front-end learning journey. That’s going to take some work and time.

My latest IPO launched, and I’m now worth one trillion dollars. Wait, wait. That’s not right.

It’s going to be hard. Advertising will certainly be needed now. But, in order to keep the lights on, I’ll have to approach them differently.

In the pre-DigitalOcean days we sold real ad packages with things like display ads, sponsored posts, newsletter sponsors, and the like. As an independent website, we will need to return to our advertising roots.

So, if you’re in a place looking to advertise, please do reach out!

Right?! Ad revenue can’t be the only thing paying the bills. I have lots of ideas and several plates already spinning. Like, I’ve been neck deep in developing an HTML/CSS course for beginners.

(And you can now support the site by becoming a member — thanks in advance!)

Educational content has been in the dumps lately, right? Again, I’m hoping that CSS-Tricks will be more inclusive, no matter where you or anyone else is at in the learning journey. This place has traditionally been for folks who know what they’re doing. But I’m truly committed to creating more entry points for those getting started, particularly as our field is in the early throes of a growing skills gap.

And yes, I do believe that learning the basics is more important now than ever. But what learning actually looks like is what’s key. Front-end courses are down across the board. It has to be done differently.

There are other things in the works, but I think a course is one of the first things you’ll see.

Oh, and the newsletter. That’s coming back after a way-too-long hiatus.

That’s still very much a thing! We’ll keep our regularly scheduled broadcast going.

But maybe a little less than you’re used to. Those articles are part of the investment I’m making. Expect maybe one or two articles most weeks, at least as the ball starts rolling.

That also means the guest writing form is back in business! We have a nice backlog of articles through the rest of the year, which is great, but also means new submissions will have to wait a bit.

I won’t be able to accept everything that comes in, but I will absolutely read them and follow-up as best I can. Unless it’s a slop submission, of course.

Nah! My goal is to make CSS-Tricks a platform that gives folks a voice to share great ideas. I think we all prefer that over me just using it as my own soapbox. I’ve written plenty for this site the past 12 years. I’m happier (and more comfortable) being behind the curtain and giving others the full spotlight.

I may be writing a bit, developing the site here and there, editing a small stack of article drafts, or helping with sponsorships. I gag at the thought of titles, but publisher hits best for me. My fingers are in all the bowls, but my most important responsibilities are:

The best thing I can possibly do is help folks develop a voice and give them a stage to use it. It’s about community and culture building. Publisher. That’s more my jam.

You totally can. I put a little membership deal together where you can join a members-only community on Discord along with some perks. Or check out the new shop. Or purchase advertising for your company. In the near-future you will be able to purchase the aforementioned course as well. Any little bit helps, of course. I squirm at the thought of asking for money, so there’s no pressure.

Support doesn’t even have to be financial! We all know that content publishers need all the eyeballs we can get in these prompt-filled days. Things like subscribing to the newsletter, sharing articles you like with others, clicking through from your RSS app, and checking out our sponsors are all very, very great ways to support us.

Yeah, me too. This post could go on and on. I’m an open book, though, and I’m happy to answer anything you might have in the comments. I know, so 2015, right?

Andy Clarke shows you how to create designs that people recognize and remember in his brand-new e-book, Standout Designs for the Web. Pre-order it today!

#10221. Sizing GPU Memory for a RAG Service: KV Cache, Context and Concurrency

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

A retrieval-augmented generation (RAG) app is usually drawn as a language model plus a vector database. When it goes live, the part that fails first is rarely the retrieval. It is GPU memory on the generation model, because a model that loads fine for one request can run out of memory when several users send long questions at once.

This article shows how to estimate that limit before you deploy, with a short script you can run, and then covers what else in the pipeline can slow responses down.

Serving a model takes more than its weights. The GPU also holds:

the key-value (KV) cache, which grows with the length of every active request

memory used by the inference runtime itself

SitePoint's guide to running a local LLM on 10GB of VRAM makes the same point for a single desktop GPU: the usable budget is the total VRAM minus overhead, and the KV cache grows with the context window, so the real model budget is smaller than the number on the box.

For a RAG service, the KV cache matters even more. Retrieved passages are pasted into the prompt, so every request carries a long context, and every concurrent request holds its own cache.

The cache size per token depends on the model's architecture: two tensors (keys and values) for every layer, for every KV head. This script turns those numbers into a rough limit on concurrent requests:

def kv_bytes_per_token(layers, kv_heads, head_dim, dtype_bytes=2):

# Keys and values, for every layer, for one token.

return 2 *layers *kv_heads *head_dim *dtype_bytes

def max_concurrent(vram_gb, weights_gb, ctx_tokens, per_token, reserve_gb=2.0):

budget = (vram_gb - weights_gb - reserve_gb) * GIB

return int(budget // (ctx_tokens * per_token))

per_token = kv_bytes_per_token(32, 8, 128)

print(per_token // 1024, "KiB per token")

n = max_concurrent(24, 16, ctx, per_token)

print(ctx, "tokens ->", n, "concurrent requests")

For a 24 GB GPU holding about 16 GB of fp16 weights with 2 GB kept in reserve, it prints:

The numbers are estimates. Real runtimes add their own overhead, and the architecture values come from the model's configuration file, so read them from the model you actually use. The pattern is what matters: doubling the context length halves the number of requests that fit. A RAG prompt that stuffs in ten retrieved chunks costs real capacity.

Serving runtimes do not simply crash when the cache is full. The vLLM documentation on optimization and tuning explains that when KV cache space is insufficient for all batched requests, the engine preempts some of them and recomputes them later, which can hurt end-to-end latency. Its suggested remedies include giving the cache more memory, reducing the number of concurrent sequences or batched tokens, or sharding the model across GPUs.

So the symptom of undersizing is often not an error. It is slow, uneven responses under load. Test with realistic prompt lengths and several simultaneous users, not a single request.

Embedding and ingestion have a different profile from generation. Embedding is often lighter than serving a large language model, and some teams run it on CPU. Document parsing, chunking and tokenization also run on CPU, so a fast GPU cannot make up for a slow CPU upstream. SitePoint's tutorial on a local RAG system for private documents walks through chunking, embeddings, vector storage and reranking, and is a good map of which stages run where.

RAM: vector indexes, document loaders and caches all live in system memory, so large collections need more of it.

Storage: model files, indexes, logs and backups add up. Fast storage shortens model loading and re-indexing.

Network: if the vector database, application server and model server sit in different places, latency adds to every request.

Two layouts are common. A single instance runs everything and is simplest for prototypes and internal tools. Separated services put ingestion and retrieval on smaller machines and keep the GPU for generation, which scales better as traffic grows.

Memory estimates only hold if the GPU memory is really yours. On a shared or time-sliced GPU, other workloads can take memory and compute away from your service, which makes capacity hard to plan. Hosting providers describe this in different terms, such as passthrough, vGPU, MIG and time-slicing. For a view of how one GPU hosting provider explains those allocation methods, GPU Mart's GPU virtualization guide compares them. It is a hosting company's page, so confirm any provider's claims about isolation and performance with your own load test.

Whatever provider you choose, ask these questions:

Is the physical GPU assigned to you exclusively, or partitioned or time-sliced?

Do you have root access to install your own drivers, CUDA libraries and containers?

Which minimum commitments, regions and support terms apply?

A small stack can run as a handful of containers: an API service for request handling and prompt assembly, an ingestion worker, a vector database and a model server. Keeping them separate lets you monitor each one's memory and CPU use and move the heavy one to bigger hardware without rewriting the rest.

For a service that stays up all month, a fixed monthly term is easier to forecast than hourly capacity. Hourly capacity suits testing quantization settings or processing a one-off document batch.

Is GPU memory always the bottleneck for RAG?

Not always. Generation usually hits memory first, but slow parsing, a small CPU or a distant vector database can dominate response time.

Does quantization solve the memory problem?

It shrinks the weights, but the KV cache still grows with context length and concurrency. Test with your real prompt sizes.

It depends on volume. Small workloads can run on CPU, while a large ingestion job may benefit from a GPU for a limited time.

Size a RAG service for the worst realistic moment, not the single-request demo. Estimate the KV cache from the model's architecture, remember that long retrieved contexts reduce how many requests fit, watch for preemption under load, keep CPU, RAM, storage and network in the plan, and find out whether the GPU is exclusive or shared. The script above is small, but it turns a vague worry about memory into numbers you can check.

#10220. What’s !important #19: CSS Refraction Effects, Scroll State Queries, @custom-media, and More

We’re back! Don’t worry, this isn’t a trick (despite what our name suggests!). However, due to the hiatus, we’re also a little behind. That’s why this issue of What’s !important is absolutely stacked. All killer, no filler. Here’s what you missed.

By the way, before we dive in, Mat Marquis’ highly recommended JavaScript for Everyone course is currently -30% off (code: CSSTRICKSFRIENDS). You don’t want to miss this.

Preethi Sam demonstrated how to create background image refraction effects with CSS. The effects are stunning, can be animated, don’t need very much CSS (no JavaScript), and we don’t need to duplicate the image either.

Brecht De Ruyte demonstrated how to build customizable components using container style queries and advanced attr(), showcasing some incredible interactive demos, including the two below:

Dan Holloran showed us how to use the different scroll-state() queries (scrollable, scrolled, snapped, and stuck). It’s not a long read, which is exactly what you want for a new but simple feature like this.

A quick, no-context CSS snippet to entice you:

Declan Chidlow shared some of the craziest CSS to ever exist, and I mean craziest. Most of it comes from Internet Explorer, but let’s face it, you knew that already. Thankfully, they don’t build them like they used to.

Bramus demonstrated how to animate CSS grids using anchor positioning. The idea is that we anchor content to its corresponding cell, and then as the grid reflows, it’s the content (not the cell) that animates with it.

(But it doesn’t work in Firefox due to what sounds like a bug.)

Chris Coyier talked about a bunch of new HTML features, and honestly, there’s a whole lot in there that you definitely haven’t heard of. Like, I’m certain of it.

Victor Ayomipo contemplated why container query adoption has been so terrible despite them topping CSS wishlists for years. Victor thinks that we’re using them wrong, so he’s here to set the record straight.

Cory LaViska demonstrated how to use @custom-media in addition to container style queries as a fallback.

Also, don’t forget that Declan has an extremely thorough @custom-media deep-dive in our Almanac.

Ahmad Shadeed tackled an interesting topic — detecting overlapping elements (with CSS, of course). If you’re keen on anchor positioning, scroll-driven animations, and again (!), container style queries, then this walkthrough is for you.

I want to know when the decorative element hits the section from the left edge, taking into consideration that the element might have a fluid position or size that changes with the viewport size or container size. Basically, it’s a dynamic problem. It’s not about using a media query to hide the decorative element, I want to hide it upon overlapping.

Maybe container queries are finally picking up?

Patrick Brosset walked us through a proposal for the previewsrc HTML attribute, which would enable us to supply preview images for images that haven’t yet loaded using only HTML. For example, we could supply a blurry preview image. However, I’d rather have something like :loaded/:not(:loaded) so that we can style/transition unloaded images with CSS rather than needing to create extra images.

Temani Afif shared a custom borders demo, where the trick is to declare a transparent border, use border-shape to define its shape, then declare a background in addition to background-clip: border-area so that it only shows up within the border, like this (requires Chrome at the moment):

In a similar manner, Leon Arnott shared this awesome trick for animating the background color of ::selection, something that’s actually forbidden (or so we thought, anyway…):

Today in CSS: We all know you can change the highlight colour of selected text, but it's impossible to animate that colour. Unless… the colour was just a variable inherited from the parent element, using @​property to make it animatable… Hahahahaha… [image or embed]

And that’s it, really. Simply use var(--bg-color) on ::selection or some other visually restrictive element.

Chris Coyier demonstrated how to random()ly animate an element along the edge of another element. There’s a cute doggo, by the way. Probably should’ve led with that.

Polypane explained how we might disable the root scroller accidentally (which is surprisingly easy to do — I’ve definitely done it), what we lose in doing so, and what to do instead.

Also, have you ever completely misunderstood how the root (:root/html) works? This is a solid explainer for that, too.

Speaking of accidents, have you ever tapped on a target twice, apparently in quick succession, and ended up zooming in? Andy Bell revealed that touch-action: manipulation disables the double-tap-to-zoom-in functionality. It isn’t new, but I don’t see many web developers using it. One for the CSS reset, I think?

Chrome and Firefox now ship every two weeks. The inaugural releases were fairly exciting (not smaller, as I assumed they’d be). In addition, Safari 27 (along with iOS, iPadOS, and macOS 27) shipped with some of the same features as Firefox, making them Baseline in the same release window. High fives all around!

Andy Clarke shows you how to create designs that people recognize and remember in his brand-new e-book, Standout Designs for the Web. Pre-order it today!

For the CSS grid animation with anchor positioning, there is indeed a Firefox bug, tracked at https://bugzilla.mozilla.org/show_bug.cgi?id=1924226

#10218. Unlocking Stable On-Policy RL on TPU with Bitwise-Zero Train–Inference Mismatch

Reinforcement learning (RL) for large language models is notoriously sensitive to numerical instability: even tiny discrepancies between rollout generation and gradient updates can destabilize training or stall convergence. In LLM RL, the model serves as the policy, π(action | previous tokens), and true on-policy learning requires every action in an update to be generated and evaluated by that exact same policy. While a synchronous loop ensures each rollout batch is scored using the same weights that generated it, a critical numerical requirement remains: the sampler and trainer must evaluate that policy identically.

In practice, production RL stacks run sampling and training through separate, specialized execution paths. The sampler chooses each action token during incremental decoding and records its behavior logprob. The trainer evaluates the same token in batched forward-backward passes to compute its current logprob and gradients. Under the same weights, these differing execution paths can still assign different logprobs to the same token and context. The training schedule is on-policy, but the numerical execution has introduced a hidden off-policy gap that compounds over multi-turn trajectories.

Our zero train–inference mismatch (Zero-TIM) implementation, developed in Tunix and verified at multi-host scale across 64 TPU v5p chips (DP8×TP8), closes this numerical gap. At the same weights, the sampler and trainer assign bitwise-identical logprobs to the same action and token history—bringing state-of-the-art, true on-policy stability to TPU RL training.

A reduction is a tree of floating-point additions. Floating-point addition is not associative, so changing the tree changes where rounding occurs. The effect is easier to expose in bfloat16, which retains less precision than float32.

The following bfloat16 example isolates the effect of reduction order:

The same issue appears in model reductions: matrix products accumulate partial sums, normalization computes statistics, and tensor-parallel shards combine partial outputs. In our original stack, sampler decode and trainer forward used different token-array shapes and forward implementations. JAX specializes computations to their input shapes, and the training forward also runs inside a differentiated program.

Across these paths, reduction order and intermediate precision were not consistently aligned. Floating-point rounding turned those arithmetic differences into different logprob bits. The weights and model equations were identical; the arithmetic was not.

The contract is simple: across sampler decode, serving prefill, and trainer forward, all three paths must produce bitwise-identical logprobs for every sampled action—comparing raw floating-point bytes under the same rollout weights, token history, and sampling settings, with zero rounding or tolerance.

Why three paths instead of two? In a decoupled RL stack, a direct sampler–trainer disagreement shows that a numerical gap exists, but not where it enters. Introducing serving prefill as a middle checkpoint turns alignment into a systematic two-step diagnostic that any RL stack can adopt:

Sampler decode vs. prefill verifies that the serving engine agrees with itself between incremental token generation and full-sequence re-scoring, while prefill vs. trainer verifies that the serving and training engines agree across compilation and differentiation boundaries.

Achieving Zero-TIM does not require merging the sampler and trainer into a single monolithic engine. Instead, we use a generalizable four-part recipe that bridges separate serving and training pipelines across multi-host TPU meshes: locking within-shard kernel accumulation, ordering cross-shard reductions, standardizing the logprob scoring tail, and wrapping serving kernels with custom differentiation rules.

Inside each tensor-parallel rank, batch-invariant Pallas kernels control accumulation order and intermediate precision. Projection kernels traverse fixed contraction blocks in order, keeping the reduction blocking independent of token-row count. Partial products accumulate in float32, with explicit output casts.

The production kernel initializes its scratch accumulator before the tile traversal, then applies this core to each tile:

The next tile continues from the float32 accumulator, not the cast output. This construction covers the attention and MLP projections and the LM head. Dedicated kernels fix RMSNorm reductions and SwiGLU’s intermediate casts. The existing Pallas paged-attention forward is retained.

For row-parallel projections, each tensor-parallel rank produces a partial output. We separate communication from addition: ppermute transports the partials, then every rank adds them in the same source-rank order.

The reduction order is explicit in the program rather than selected by a collective implementation.

Alignment must extend beyond model logits to the probability of the sampled action. Decode, prefill, and trainer apply matching sampling transforms, use the same fixed Pallas log-softmax construction, and select the recorded action token.

Prefill and trainer score the action generated during rollout rather than choosing another action. We also preserve sampled token IDs across environment turns so that every path evaluates the same token history.

The trainer reuses the serving prefill computation. Custom vector–Jacobian products retain the serving kernels for forward evaluation and provide their backward rules. The matmul example shows this separation:

The differentiable replica mirrors the kernel’s accumulation and casts, but is used only to construct the pullback. Forward values still come from the serving Pallas kernel.

Training places that forward inside a differentiated JAX program. We pass --xla_allow_excess_precision=false through XLA_FLAGS to the compiler process, constraining excess intermediate precision in the operations surrounding the Pallas kernels.

Long trajectories are evaluated in fixed-size chunks, carrying the paged KV cache forward. All action logprobs feed the complete train-batch RL loss. The reverse pass propagates gradients through those cache connections and combines parameter-gradient contributions before the optimizer update.

Across the AI systems community, eliminating train–inference mismatch has rapidly become a state-of-the-art requirement for stabilizing large-scale RL convergence. Recent open-source efforts—including vLLM with TorchTitan and RadixArk/LMSys Miles—have demonstrated how batch-invariant kernels and bitwise numerical alignment prevent off-policy drift during policy optimization.

Our work delivers an open-source, modular JAX/XLA and Pallas implementation of this contract for TPU in Tunix. Verified across a multi-host 64 TPU v5p (DP8×TP8) mesh, these kernel and collective patterns provide a reusable blueprint that teams can adopt to achieve bit-exact on-policy RL across distributed JAX/TPU stacks.

To benchmark our TPU kernels, we run a 200-step numerical verification test on the synthetic FrozenLake environment using a public Qwen3-8B checkpoint as a baseline test case using 64 TPU v5p chips with a DP8×TP8 mesh. The recipe uses a GSPO-token loss with leave-one-out advantages. We compare Standard training with trainer-old logprobs and no additional sampler correction, token-level truncated importance sampling (TIS), and Zero-TIM.

The Zero-TIM configuration also uses token-in/token-out (TiTO) handling to preserve sampled token IDs across environment turns. The comparison runs retain their existing tokenization paths.

Zero-TIM records zero mean absolute sampler–trainer logprob difference at every logged step in this window, while its training solve rate increases. Standard and TIS retain nonzero differences: TIS adjusts their contribution to the update without removing the underlying mismatch. Zero-TIM reaches higher observed solve rates in these runs.

We developed Zero-TIM prototype as part of Tunix, Google's open-source, JAX-native library for LLM RL and post-training on Cloud TPU. The kernel patterns and three-way verification contract are modular by design—making them straightforward to adopt across existing multi-host JAX/TPU serving and training pipelines—and we are actively extending support to linear-attention and Mixture-of-Experts (MoE) architectures.

Multi-Turn Agentic Reinforcement Learning with Tunix and Gemma 4 on Google TPUs

Distributed LLM Training Across Metros: Streaming DiLoCo in MaxText

Securing Your Agents: A Hands-On Guide to Agent Anomaly Detection

#10216. The Frankenstein Table

It was naivety, but when I first came across Flexbox, and then CSS Grid, I had this feeling all those positioning properties (align-self, justify-content…) would be available to use on block and inline elements; things like tables, lists, fixed elements, everything. The workings might be different under the hood, depending on the reference boxes, but the syntax and expected outcomes would be same: push to one edge, keep at the center, spread around evenly, etc.

Although that didn’t happen, what if I made something of that sort?

For example, I could take out the soul of an HTML table and stitch it back together with CSS Grid. In other words, it reads like a table, but acts like a grid, allowing me to use grid properties. Believe it or not, some form of this was used in the past to make tables responsive.

Before I kill off a table in this article though: there are a lot of advantages to straight-up tables. They provide a clean semantic layout that browsers perfectly understand and translate for assistive technology when needed. There’s even a element.

On the CSS side of things, there are indeed circumstances where CSS Grid is necessary for its intrinsic responsiveness, source order independence, and positioning. That’s why it’s an indispensable tool for layouts. Use that and let HTML tables to do the work of actually displaying tabular data!

But there’s the presentational benefit of table we should consider. Technically,

is not a presentational element like a
. It’s semantic: the browser and I share a common meaning for it. But whether the browser understands it as such or not, if I read
I believe it’s a heading followed by data. Let’s say that’s pseudo-semantic to me, if presentation is not the right term.

That pseudo-semanticism is still useful. It’s the difference between me having to read this:

Despite there being semantic elements for titles, like

and , they don’t bring in the connection that

and have, and it’s better to reserve
and
for textual structuring like glossaries rather than layouts.

So, is it okay if I use

this way? I mean, the table itself merely used as pseudo-semantic markup skin:

Then, this CSS and its outcome (for the first markup):

To me, the latter markup example — a pseudo-semantic table — is easier to scan than a handful of generic divs. I get the pseudo-semantics of table, and the ease of using CSS Grid for the layout. As for browsers, none of them have shunned this sort of Frankenstein creation, at least yet. The VoiceOver and keyboard navigation works as if it’s a real table.

But it’s understandable why it looks more than a little gross and against the grain of what we should be able to. We’re encouraged to maintain a separation of concerns between content and presentation and this does exactly that without affecting the tables semantics, such as its role. But maybe it should? There are plenty of other HTML elements that CSS is capable of busting.

Andy Clarke shows you how to create designs that people recognize and remember in his brand-new e-book, Standout Designs for the Web. Pre-order it today!

Interesting concept. I like the idea of building out a data/stats card (say, one you’d see in the header of a CRUD edit/view page) with this method.

yes this is indeed scary and i would not use it for these reasons:

– headings are more appropriate in this layout (slap a list or group on it if you want to be more verbose) – changing the display alone already stripped the soul (semantic) of the table – pseudo-semantic is only a developer convenience, not for the end users (VoiceOver is only one of many screen readers, not enough to confirm it works like a real table)

I would suggest a “ in your example. Also the bank does not have a label, correct would be `IssuerName of the bank`. So most of the time there are other ways before using a table with all its markup.

Ha! HTML elements got stripped. So I meant a definition list (dl) and the “Issuer” would be the “th” and “Name of the bank” would be the “td”.

You don’t need to resort to misusing HTML tables for this layout. Your original HTML version could be much improved by simply using sections, headings and paragraphs. In addition, you can also hand over the all-caps and line-breaks styling to CSS while you’re at it. No divs, spans, nor incorrect use of the strong element.

The pseudo-semantic angle is what really sold me here. I run an informational niche site that’s full of reference tables (status codes, payment schedules), and scanning raw markup with / pairs is so much easier to read than a pile of nested divs. That said, Mike Mai’s point about VoiceOver gives me pause — I’ll probably keep this trick for decorative layouts and leave the actual data tables semantically intact.

This is a fun experiment, and I especially liked the distinction you drew between a real table and a pseudo-semantic one. Once display: grid is applied, the browser no longer treats the th and td relationship the way it does in a normal table, so the visual connection stays for sighted readers while the structural meaning is lost. That trade-off is what makes Mike Mai’s point land for me: it is convenient for the author, but assistive technology does not get the same benefit. I would keep this pattern for a purely presentational card only where the same information is also available in a straightforward, accessible form, rather than using it for actual data.

This is a fun experiment, and I especially liked the distinction you drew between a real table and a pseudo-semantic one. Once display: grid is applied, the browser no longer treats the th and td relationship the way it does in a normal table, so the visual connection stays for sighted readers while the structural meaning is lost. That trade-off is what makes Mike Mai’s point land for me: it is convenient for the author, but assistive technology does not get the same benefit. I would keep this pattern for a purely presentational card only where the same information is also available in a straightforward, accessible form, rather than using it for actual data.

I’m curious as to why you avoided using a DL here. MDN says “Common uses for this element are to implement a glossary or to display metadata (a list of key-value pairs).” These days you can also wrap the dt/dd in div elements to more easily style these pairs.

As for browsers, none of them have shunned this sort of Frankenstein creation, at least yet.

They have for years. It was such a common barrier I made a function in 2018 to try to restore some table semantics. While it took years for browsers to catch up, even in 2022 tables with display properties were a mess and, unsurprisingly, there are still regressions and new bugs. Using display: contents is equally risky, and this is partly because it wasn’t well specified (meaning the spec did a poor job and browser vendors didn’t recognize all the gaps).

In addition, this is not a well-formed table. A linter or other automated code checker should call this table out for missing a cell in row two:

The experience for a screen reader use navigating down the right column might be that they never hear that cell (and I assume that brand is important if it’s included). The user may also get incorrect row and column cell counts.

I encourage readers to test with more than VoiceOver, and additionally to test with daily screen reader users. Further, given the years of display regressions (looking at Safari in particular), to re-test this construct for every new browser release.

#10214. What’s !important #20: GTA 6 Scroll Effects, Scrollspies, random()

This is the final issue of What’s !important on CSS-Tricks.com. The good news is that it’s coming back in an expanded form as the CSS-Tricks newsletter. I had ya there for a second, didn’t I? Sorry, I couldn’t decide whether to trick or treat, so I went for both. Happy Halloween!

The newsletter officially kicks off on November 10th. That’s after a way-too-long four-year hiatus. What the heck, right? It’s not only high time for it to come back, but for you to get on the list. You’ll get hand-written and hand-curated news, links, and editorials that you can only find there. Join the party with me!

So, let’s go out with a big bang for our “final” issue. It’s packed with some stuff that I’ve been holding onto since before the site came back to life earlier this week, but also some new stuff, and I can’t wait to finally get it out to you.

Scooch over Apple, Rockstar has something they’d like to say about fancy scrolling effects (to be fair, they’ve had 13 years to create them). First, check out the GTA 6 website (which oddly doesn’t work in Firefox), then watch Kevin Powell’s GTA 6 scroll effects walkthrough, which is frankly the most fun that you can have while waiting (and waiting) for GTA 6.

Mojtaba Seyedi demonstrated how to build a scrollspy with modern CSS. What’s a scrollspy, you ask? Well, if you have a navigation that links to different sections of the page, a scrollspy highlights the section that you’re currently on in the navigation.

Brecht De Ruyte showcased and explained some absolutely stunning random() demos.

Lea Verou explained, in detail, the different ways of implementing icons on the web. Many exciting CSS features of the future are mentioned, including Linked Parameters, ident(), @mixin and @apply, the @property shorthand, and more, so it’s a really deep dive with a lot of intrigue.

Kilian Valkhof suggested a universal pseudo-selector. I mean, the universal selector (*) doesn’t select pseudo-elements, which is why almost every website uses the following CSS snippet, right?

But there are way more of them now (e.g., ::backdrop and ::details-content, to name just a couple of common ones in the context of box-sizing: border-box), so Kilian suggests ::*.

Declan Chidlow told us which new HTML and CSS features are useful for design systems. My favorites are text-box, sibling-index(), sibling-count(), @scope, and @custom-media, which are very handy even outside of design systems.
